## Page 1


Masures associated with split Kac-Moody groups over valued
fields
Auguste Hébert
Université de Lorraine, Institut Élie Cartan de Lorraine, F-54000 Nancy, France
UMR 7502, auguste.hebert@univ-lorraine.fr
arXiv:2604.15943v1  [math.GR]  17 Apr 2026


## Page 2


Abstract
Masures are generalizations of Bruhat-Tits buildings adapted to the study of Kac–Moody
groups over valued fields. They were introduced by Gaussent and Rousseau in 2007. Rousseau
defined an axiomatic for these object and we simplified it. In this paper, which is mainly
expository, we construct the masure associated with a split Kac–Moody group over a valued
field, and we prove that it satisfies our axiomatic.

Introduction
Kac–Moody groups, masures and applications
Kac–Moody groups
Kac–Moody groups are interesting generalizations of reductive group which integrate Kac–
Moody (Lie) algebras, in some sense. There are several possible definitions of Kac–Moody
groups, which mainly fall into two categories: the minimal ones and the completed ones. An
example of a minimal Kac–Moody group is SLn(K[t, t−1]). The maximal Kac–Moody group
associated is SLn(K((t))), for K a field. Here we are mainly interested in minimal Kac–Moody
groups as defined by Tits in [Tit87], but we also use Mathieu’s completion as a tool to study
them.
A split Kac–Moody group G is a functor from the category of rings to the category of
groups. It admits a split maximal torus T and a set of real roots Φ. If K is a field, then
G := G(K) is generated by T and root subgroups Uα, for α ∈Φ, where for each α ∈Φ, Uα
is isomorphic to (K, +). Note that unless G is reductive, Φ is infinite and thus G is infinite
dimensional.
For example if G = SLn(K), one can choose T as the subgroup of diagonal matrices, and
one can choose the root subgroups to be the subgroups Ui,j(K) with ones on the diagonal,
a coefficient in K in position (i, j), and 0 elsewhere, with (i, j) ∈J1, nK, i ̸= j.
If G =
SLn(K[t, t−1]), one can choose the torus to be the diagonal matrices with coefficients in K∗,
and the root groups to be the Ui,j,k = Ui,j(tkK), with i, j as above and k ∈Z. There are three
types of indecomposable Kac–Moody groups: finite type, affine type and indefinite type, and
the same hold for the associated Kac–Moody groups. Apart from the finite type ones, which
are reductive, the most studied and understood Kac–Moody groups are the untwisted affine
groups. Up to central extensions and semi-direct product with (K×)n for some n ∈N, there
are of the form K 7→˚G(K[t, t−1]) for ˚G a reductive group. Indefinite Kac–Moody groups are
more mysterious. They are not linear in general (see [Mar18, 9.3] for precise statements) and
no “explicit” realization of them are known.
The study of Kac–Moody groups over non-Archimedean local fields began in the affine
case with the works of Garland ([Gar95]). Braverman and Kazhdan then initiated the theory
of Hecke algebras of affine Kac–Moody groups over local fields ([BK11]) and Gaussent and
4


## Page 7


Rousseau generalized their works in the general affine case, using the theory of masures, that
they initiated in [GR08].
Buildings and groups
In order to study reductive and Kac–Moody groups, one can associate “buildings” to them.
They are objects of geometric and combinatorial nature on which the groups under study act.
One can then translate questions on the group in questions on the associated building. There
are several types of buildings. Let G be a reductive group (functor), that we assume to be
split for simplicity. If K is any field, then the Tits building of G(K) is a simplicial complex
equipped with an action of G(K).
If F is a field equipped with a non-trivial real-valued valuation ω : F →R ∪{+∞}, one
can then associate a Bruhat–Tits building I to G(F) (this was done by Bruhat and Tits in
[BT72] and [BT84]). The Tits building of G(F) can then be regarded as a boundary at infinity
of I. Let k be the residual field of (F, ω). Then the Tits building of G(k) can be regarded
as the tangent building at some vertex of I. Let B be a Borel subgroup of G. For example
if G = SLn, with n ∈Z≥2, one can take B to be the subfunctor of G consisting of the upper
triangular matrices. The Iwahori subgroup KI of G(F), is proj−1
k (B(k)) ⊂G(O), if O is the
ring of integers of F and projk : G(O) →G(k) is the natural projection. The Tits building of
G(K) is a kind of geometric realization of G(K)/B(K), and if ω is discrete, we can regard I
as a geometric realization of G(F)/KI.
Consider now a split Kac–Moody group G. If K is a field, the analogue of the Tits building
of G(K) is a twin building. This is a pair (I+
K, I−
K) of buildings, equipped with a codistance
(a twinning) between I+
K and I−
K. The fact that there are naturally two buildings instead of
one comes from the fact that there are two conjugacy classes of Borel subgroups, contrary
to the reductive case. If B+ and B−are two opposite Borel subgroup, i.e there are Borel
subgroups whose intersection is a maximal split torus, then one can take I+
K = G(K)/B+(K)
and I−
K = G(K)/B−(K). Let (F, ω) be as above. The object corresponding to the Bruhat–
Tits building of (G, F, ω) is the masure I. At infinity of the masure, we find the twin building
of G(F), and the tangent building at some vertex is the twin building of G(k).
Masures and applications
Masures enabled to prove many results in the theory of Kac–Moody groups over non-Archimedean
local fields. To cite but a few, it enabled progresses in the following fields:
• In the theory of MV-cycles for Kac–Moody groups, see [GR08], [BG23] and [BV25].
Bouthier and Vasserot indirectly use masures in their work on the geometric Satake
equivalence, since they use finiteness results proved using this object.
• In the theory of Hecke algebras for Kac–Moody groups over valued fields. It enabled
Bardy-Panse, Gaussent and Rousseau to define a spherical Hecke algebra ([GR14]) and
an Iwahori–Hecke algebra ([BGR16]) in general, extending the constructions of Braver-
man, Kazhdan and Patnaik in the untwisted affine case (see [BK11] and [BKP16]).
Hébert and Abdellatif also associated Hecke algebras to certain parahoric subgroups in
[AH19].
• In Kazhdan–Lusztig theory for Kac–Moody groups, it enabled Muthiah, Hébert and
Philippe to give a conjectural definition of Kazhdan–Lusztig R-polynomials ([Mut19]
and [HP24]), based on a theory of twin masures ([BPHR25]).

Axioms of masures
Bruhat–Tits buildings
Let (F, ω) be a valued field, G be a split reductive group or Kac–Moody group and G = G(F).
We now describe how to construct the Bruhat–Tits building of G or its masure and what are
its properties.
First assume that G is reductive. Let T be a maximal split torus of G. Let Y be the
cocharacter lattice of (G, T) and let A = Y ⊗R be the standard apartment. Let N be the
normalizer of T = T(F) in G. Then N naturally acts on A by affine automorphisms. Let Φ
be the root system of (G, T). For α ∈Φ, we have an isomorphism xα : F →Uα.
The Bruhat-Tits building I = I(G, F, ω) is defined as follows.
For x ∈A, one sets
Gx = ⟨Nx, Uα,x | α ∈Φ⟩⊂G, where Nx is the fixator of x in N and Uα,x = xα({a ∈F |
α(x) + ω(a) ≥0}) for α ∈Φ. For example G0 = G(O). Then I is defined as (G × A)/ ∼, for
some equivalence relation ∼such that for all x ∈G, Gx is the fixator of x in A, where A is
regarded as a subset of I via the embedding A ,→I, a 7→[a, 1] (see Subsection 6.1).
For example, if G = SLn, with n ∈N≥2, then N is the group of monomial matrices and
Φ can be written Φ = {αi,j | (i, j) ∈J1, nK}. For i, j ∈J1, nK one can define xαi,j as follows.
For a ∈F, xαi,j(a) = In + aEi,j, where Ei,j is the matrix with a one in position (i, j) and 0
elsewhere. The subgroup Gx is computed in Proposition 1.5.1, when n = 2.
The set I is then covered by its apartments, where an apartment is a set of the form g.A,
for some g ∈G. Let Φ be the root system of (G, T). Then A is equipped with the hyperplane
arrangement H = {α−1({k}) | k ∈ω(F×)} whose elements are called walls. Then, using the
action of G, we can equip every apartment with its wall arrangement. A half-apartment is
then a half-space of an apartment delimited by a wall. When ω(F×) is discrete, H naturally
equips A with the structure of a CW-complex. Then I is an affine R-building in the sense of
[Rou23, §2.4.4]. In particular, it satisfies the following two properties:
(I1) for every two faces, there exists an apartment A containing these two faces,
(I2) for every two apartments A, A′, A ∩A′ is a finite intersection of half-apartments of A
and there exists g ∈G such that g.A = A′ and g fixes A ∩A′.
Note that property (I1) is the building’s theoretic translation of the Bruhat decomposition
G = GF · N · GF ′, for every two faces F, F ′ of A, where GF and GF ′ are respectively the
fixators of F and F ′ in G and N is the normalizer of T(F) in G. For example, when G = SL2,
(A, H) ≃(R, Z) and I is a homogeneous tree of valency |k| + 1, where k is the residue field of
(F, ω) (see Figure 0.0.1). When G = SL3, A ≃R2 and the walls of A form a tessellation of A
with regular triangles (see Figure 0.0.2). The building is then a union of apartments, which
intersect along walls, see Figure 0.0.3).
Masures
Let G be a maximal split reductive group and let F be a field equipped with a non-trivial
valuation ω. Let T be a maximal split torus, G = G(F) and T = T(F). Let A = Y ⊗R, where
Y is the cocharacter lattice of (G, T). In order to define the masure I of G, we proceed as in
the reductive case. We define a group Gx for each x ∈R, and we then define I as G × A/ ∼,
for some equivalence relation ∼such that Gx is the fixator of x in A. The definition of ∼is
the same as in the reductive case, the difficulty is to define the Gx, for x ∈A.
One defines an action of N on A by affine automorphisms. This enables to define the
fixator Nx, for every x ∈A. This defines the N-component of Gx, similarly as in the reductive
case. To define the whole Gx however, we use the positive and negative Mathieu’s completions
of G.

Another difficulty in the Kac–Moody case is that (unless G is reductive), Φ is infinite and
thus the hyperplane arrangement H = {α−1({k}) | k ∈ω(F×)} can be dense, even if ω(F×)
is discrete (see Figures 0.0.4 and 0.0.5). Therefore H does not equip A with the structure of
a CW-complex. A solution to this issue is to define the faces of A as filters on A (instead of
subsets of A). Note that this solution is already used in the reductive case, when ω(F×) is
not discrete.
Then I is the union of its apartments, which are the g.A for g ∈G. We prove that I
satisfies the following two axioms (see Definition 4.1.6 and Theorem 6.7.13):
(MA2) Let A and B be two apartments.
Then there exists finitely many half-apartments
D1, . . . , Dn of A such that A ∩B = Tn
i=1 Di and there exists g ∈G such that g.A = B
and g fixes A ∩B.
(MA3) Let R1 be a splayed chimney germ and R2 be a face or a chimney germ. Then there
exists an apartment A containing R1 and R2.
Axiom (MA2) improves and replaces some of the axioms defined by Rousseau in [Rou11].
Note that it involves only half-apartments defined by real roots (and not imaginary) although
the construction of I uses Mathieu’s completion of G, which involves imaginary roots. Axiom

Note that for simplicity, we did not
Chronology of the theory of masures
Masures were introduced by Gaussent and Rousseau in [GR08]. In this paper, they are associ-
ated with split Kac–Moody groups over discretely valued fields whose residual fields contains
C. Rousseau obtained a construction valid for any field F equipped with an R-valued valuation
in [Rou16]. Rousseau also defined masures associated with almost-split Kac–Moody groups in
[Rou17], using works of Charignon ([Cha10]).
Rousseau gave an axiomatic definition of masures in [Rou11] and proved that masures
associated with Kac–Moody groups satisfy its axioms. We simplified this definition in [Héb20]
(in the affine case) and in general in [Héb22]: we proved that under a freeness assumption on
the simple coroots, his axioms are equivalent to (MA2) and (MA3).
Here we prove directly that I satisfies these axioms. We start from scratch: we recall
most of the necessary facts on Kac–Moody groups and we do not assume that the reader is
familiar with the theory of Bruhat–Tits buildings or masures. We restrict to the case of split
Kac–Moody groups, except in Chapter 4.

New results of this paper
The aim of this paper is to give a self-contained construction of the masure associated to a
split Kac–Moody group and to give a direct proof that it satisfies (MA2) and (MA3). While
it is mainly expository, we obtain a few new results that we list here.
Filters have good fixators
For V a filter on A, we denote by GV its fixator in G. When they defined masures in [GR08],
Gaussent and Rousseau introduced the notion of “good fixator”, which enabled them to prove
many useful properties of masures. The filter V is said to have a good fixator if GV acts
transitively on the set of apartments containing V and if GV decomposes as:
GV = (U+ ∩GV) · (U−∩GV) · (N ∩GV) = (U−∩GV) · (U+ ∩GV) · (N ∩GV).
We prove that actually, every filter on V has a good fixator (see Theorem 6.6.7).
Hecke paths
Most of the applications of the theory of masures to Kac–Moody groups over local fields use
Hecke paths. If x ∈I, the retraction ρ+∞(x) of x on A along U+ is the unique element
of U+.x ∩A. In [GR08, Theorem 6.2], the authors prove that if x, y ∈I are preordered (a
condition defined in [Rou16, 5.8]), then ρ+∞([x, y]) is a Hecke path, which means that it is a
piecewise affine path satisfying precise folding conditions (see Definition 4.6.2). We generalize
this result by dropping the assumption on x and y (see Theorem 4.6.13). While for all the
current applications in arithmetic, the segment is assumed to be preordered, this generalization
is useful in our proof that I satisfies (MA2).
On the axiom (MA2)
In [Héb22], we proved that I satisfies (MA2), provided that the sets of simple roots and the
sets of simple coroots are free families. We drop the freeness assumption on the set of simple
co-roots. To do so, we separate the affine case and the indefinite case, and use arguments of
[Héb20] and [Héb22].

Description of the fixator of a spherical local face
Let C be an alcove of A. In [BPHR25, 2.4], it is proved that GC = ⟨NC, Uα,C | α ∈Φ⟩,
where NC = T(O) is the fixator of C in N. We slightly generalize this result here. We prove
that if F is a spherical local face of A (i.e the fixator of its direction in W v is finite), then
GF = ⟨NF , Uα,F | α ∈Φ⟩(see Proposition 7.1.7).
Organization of the paper
This paper is organized as follows.
In Chapter 1, we construct and describe the tree of SL2, which is the “simplest” possible
example of masure. This chapter is intended to give an intuition of what a masure is and is
mainily independent of the others.
In Chapter 2, we define and study Kac–Moody algebras. We then describe the vectorial
apartments associated, which are the standard apartments of the masure equipped with the
walls containing 0.
In Chapter 3, we introduce minimal and completed Kac–Moody groups.
In Chapter 4, we introduce abstract axioms of masures. We then study masures from an
axiomatic viewpoint and prove that a series of axioms implies (MA2). Masures of the chapter
are not associated with a group. In particular, our results also apply to masures associated
with almost-split Kac–Moody groups.
In Chapter 5, we define and study the fixators Gx, for x ∈A, when G = G(F) is a split
Kac–Moody group over a valued field.
We define the action of N on A and we associate
subgroups of Uma+ to filters on A.
In Chapter 6, we define the masure of G and we prove that it satisfies our axiomatic. We
also obtain descriptions of the fixator GV, for V a filter on A.
In Chapter 7, we describe the fixator GV of V, in certain particular cases, where V is a
subface of the fundamental alcove C+
0 .

General notations
The set N is the set of non-negative integers.
If a, b are in an affine space or in R∪{−∞, +∞}, we denote by [a, b[ (resp. ]a, b], ]a, b[) the
set [a, b] \ {b} (resp. [a, b] \ {a}, [a, b] \ {a, b}). If a, b ∈R, we denote by Ja, bK the set [a, b] ∩Z.
The notation “≪0” and “≫0” mean “small enough” and “big enough”. For example if Pk
is a property, for k ∈N, then Pk is true for k ≫0 if there exists N ∈N such that Pk is true
for all k ≥N.

## Page 15


Chapter 1
The tree of SL2
In this chapter, we construct and study the tree of SL2(F), when F is a real-valued field. The
aim of this chapter is to give an example of a Bruhat-Tits building in order to give an intuition
of what Bruhat-Tits buildings look like. This chapter is mainly independent of the others.
We use the “norm approach” introduced in [GI63] (when F is a local field) and used in
[Par00] (see [Par23] for an english translation) and [Rou23, 6]. We then relate it to the “lattice
approach” used in [Ser77] (see [Ser80] for an english translation) (when F is discretely valued)
and [Chi01] (for a non-necessarily real-valued valuation). All these are very particular cases of
the Bruhat-Tits buildings introduced in [BT72] and [BT84]. For simplicity, we choose to study
only SL2(F), but most of the results are easily adaptable to the case of SLn(F), for n ∈Z≥2.
We use non necessarily discrete valuations: we choose this level of generality because in this
set-up, faces are naturally filters, whereas when the valuation is discrete, they can be seen as
sets. For masures, the faces are filters even if the valuation is discrete.
1.1
Definition of the tree
Let F be a field equipped with a non-trivial valuation ω : F →R∪{+∞}. We set Λ = ω(F∗).
Let O = {x ∈F | ω(x) ≥0} be the valuation ring of (F, ω). We now construct the Bruhat-
Tits building of (SL2(F), ω) as a quotient of a space of norms on F2. This follows Goldman–
Iwahori’s and Parreau’s constructions ([GI63] and [Par00]). For a ∈F, we set |a| = e−ω(a).
We set G = SL2(F).
Definition 1.1.1. A ultrametric norm on F2 is a map η : F2 →R≥0 such that for all
u, v ∈F2, a ∈F, we have:
1. η(v) = 0 if and only if v = 0,
2. η(av) = |a|η(v),
3. η(u + v) ≤max(η(u), η(v)).
Let b = (b1, b2) be a basis of F2 A norm η is called adapted to b if we have η(λb1 +µb2) =
max(η(λb1), η(µb2)), for all λ, µ ∈F. A norm η of F2 is called adaptable if there exists a
basis with respect to which η is adapted. We denote by N the set of adaptable ultrametric
norms on F2.
Note that when F is a local field, every ultrametric norm on F2 is adaptable, by [GI63,
Proposition 1.1].
More generally, if the valuation ω is discrete and if F is Henselian and
complete, then every ultrametric norm on F2 is adaptable, by [KP23, Proposition 15.1.11].
Definition 1.1.2. Let η, η′ ∈N . We write η ∼η′ if there exists a ∈R>0 such that η′ = aη.
Then ∼is an equivalence relation. If η ∈N , we denote by [η] its equivalence class. We set
IN = N / ∼. This is the tree of (SL2(F), ω).

1.2
Apartments
In this section, we introduce the apartments of IN . These are subsets of IN , isometric to R,
which cover IN .
Apartments are a crucial tool in the study of buildings and of the groups acting on them.
For example we can translate certain decompositions of G in terms of existence of apartments
containing pairs of filters, see for instance Corollary 1.8.6 and Propositions 1.8.3 and 1.8.5.
1.2.1
Apartments
Let b be a basis of F2.
The apartment associated with b is the set Ab = {[ηb,x] | x ∈
R2/R(1, 1)}.
It is easy to check that x 7→ηb,x is a bijection between R2/R(1, 1) and Ab.
Therefore we can regard apartments as copies of R. The standard apartment A is the
apartment associated with the canonical basis e = (e1, e2) of F2. Explicitely, we identify A
and R via the map
R 7→A, x 7→[ηe,(0,x)].
(1.2.1)
An apartment of IN is a set of the form Ab, for some basis b of F2. If η ∈N , then
[η] ∈Ab, for every basis adapted to η, by (1.1.1). In particular, IN is the union of all its
apartments.
If a ∈F×, x ∈R and b = (b1, b2) is a basis of F2, we have:
ηab,x = eω(a)ηb,x, η(b1,ab2),x = ηb,x−ω(a) and η(b2,b1),x = e−xη(b1,b2),−x.
(1.2.2)
The apartments are subsets of IN which are isometric to a line: when ω is discrete, they
are bi-infinite paths of edges without u-turns (see Figure 0.0.1). When (F, ω) is complete, one
can show that the apartments are exactly the subsets of IN isometric to R.

1.3
Vertices of IN and lattice viewpoint
We now give the lattice description of the tree of G. This appears in [GI63] and this is the
viewpoint adopted in [Ser77] (where Λ = Z) and [Chi01, 4.3] (in [Chi01], ω does not necessarily
take values in R). We prove that the lattice tree can be naturally identified with the set of
vertices of IN .
A lattice of F2 is a subset of F2 of the form Ob1 ⊕Ob2, for some basis (b1, b2) of F2. We
say that two lattices L1, L2 of F2 are equivalent if there exists λ ∈F× such that L1 = λL2.
If L is a lattice of F2, we denote by [L] its equivalence class. The lattice tree IL of G with
respect to ω is the set of lattices of F2 quotiented by the equivalence relation. The group
GL2(F) (and thus G) acts on IL via g.[L] = [g.L] for g ∈GL2(F) and L a lattice of F2.
A vertex of IN is a point of IN of the form [ηb,0], for some basis b of F2. We denote by
vert(IN ) the set of vertices of IN . We set 0I = [ηe,0], where e is the canonical basis of F2.
If η is a norm on F2 and r ∈R, we set Bη(r) = {x ∈F2 | η(x) ≤e−r} and Bη(r) = {x ∈
F2 | η(x) < e−r}. For r ∈R, we set F≥r = {x ∈F | ω(x) ≥r}.

1.4

Let eF be a field extension of F and eω be a valuation on eF, with values in R. We say that

a common apartment. Therefore defining the faces as translates of intervals of A by elements
of G is not satisfactory in the non-discrete case. In order to define faces uniformly (in discrete
and dense cases), a solution is to use the notion of filters.
1.7.2
Filters and faces of IN
We now give a definition of faces which works for any valuation ω with values in R. For this
we use the notion of filters. For example, the filter ]0, 0+[, consisting of all the subsets of IN
containing ]0, ϵ[⊂A, for some ϵ ∈R>0, will be a face.
1.7.2.1
Filters
Definition 1.7.2. A filter on a set E is a nonempty set V of nonempty subsets of E such that,
for all subsets Ω, Ω′ of E, we have:
• Ω, Ω′ ∈V implies Ω∩Ω′ ∈V
• Ω′ ⊂Ωand Ω′ ∈V implies Ω∈V.
If E is a set and V, V′ are filters on E, we define V ∪V′ to be the filter {Ω∪Ω′ | (Ω, Ω′) ∈
V × V′}.
Definition 1.7.3 (filter generated by a prefilter). Let E be a non-empty set. Let B be a set
of subsets of E. We say that B is a prefilter if ∅/∈B and for all Ω1, Ω2 ∈B, there exists
Ω∈B such that Ω⊂Ω1 ∩Ω2.
If B is a prefilter, the filter generated by B is the filter on E consisting of the subsets
of E containing an element of B.
Note that if V is a filter on a set E, then it is a prefilter on E. Therefore if E′ is a set
containing E, we can consider the filter on E′ generated by V. By abuse of language, we often
identify these two filters, when there is no ambiguity. For example, we often define filters on
A and then consider them as filters on IN .
Let E, E′ be sets, f : E →E′ be a map and V be a filter on E. Then f(V) is the filter on
E′ generated by the prefilter {f(Ω) | Ω∈V}.
21


## Page 24


If V is a filter on a set E, and Ωis a subset of E, we say that V contains Ωif every element
of V contains Ω. We denote it V ⊃Ω. If Ωis nonempty, the principal filter on E associated
with Ωis the filter FΩ,E of subsets of E containing Ω.
A filter V is said to be contained in another filter V′: V ⊂V′ (resp. in a subset Z in E:
V ⊂Z) if every set in V′ is in V (resp. if Z ∈V).
These definitions of containment are inspired by the following facts. Let E be a set, V be
a filter on E and Ω, Ω′ ⊂E. Then :
• Ω⊂Ω′ if and only if FΩ,E ⊂FΩ′,E,
• Ω⊂V if and only if FΩ,E ⊂V,
• Ω⊃V if and only if FΩ,E ⊃V.
If Ωis a non-empty subset of a non-empty topological space E, and x ∈Ω, then the
germ of Ωat x is the filter germx(Ω) on E composed with the subsets of E containing a
neighborhood of x in Ω. If V is a filter on E, then V is the filter on E generated by the prefilter
{Ω| Ω∈V}.
Let E be a set and let f : E →E. Let V be a filter on E. We say that f fixes V if it
fixes pointwise at least one element of V. Assume now that f is a bijection. We say that f
stabilizes V if for every subset Ωof E, we have f(Ω) ∈V if and only if Ω∈V. In particular,
if H is a group acting on E, we can talk of the fixator and the stabilizer of the filter V. These
are subgroups of H.

Faces of A
We set α = Id : A ≃R →R and Φ = {±α}.
The fundamental vectorial chamber is Cv

of Cv

R<0).
A sector q of A is a set of the form a + Cv, for some vectorial face Cv. Its germ at
infinity, denoted Q = germ∞(q) is the filter on A consisting of the subsets of A containing
b + Cv, for some b ∈A. As A is one-dimensional, its sector-germs at infinity are usually called
the ends of A. For ϵ ∈{−, +}, we denote by Qϵ∞the end of A associated to the sector ϵ∞.
A (closed) half-apartment (resp. open half-apartment, wall) of A is a set of the form
D(β, k) := {x ∈A | β(x) + k ≥0} (resp. ˚
D(β, k) = {x ∈A | β(x) + k > 0}, M(α, k) = {k} or
M(−α, k) = −k) where β ∈{α, −α} and k ∈Λ. We set D(β, +∞) = A = ˚
D(β, +∞).
If Ωis a filter on A (in particular it can be a non-empty set), its enclosure cl(Ω) is the
filter on A consisting of the subsets E of A containing an intersection of the form D(α, kα) ∩
D(−α, k−α) containing Ω, with kα, k−α ∈Λ ∪{+∞}. A subset of A is called enclosed if it is
the intersection of finitely many (possibly zero) half-apartments of A.

1.9
Building axiomatic satisfied by IN
In this section, we introduce certain axioms of affine R-buildings and prove that they are
satisfied by IN . They are similar to the axioms of masures, but an important difference is
axiom (A3’), which implies that two points are always contained in a common apartment, and
which is false in general for masures.

1.9.1
Axioms (A2) to (A4)
An apartment of type (A, f
W) is a set A equipped with a non-empty set Isom(A, A) of
bijections from A to A such that if f0 ∈Isom(A, A), then Isom(A, A) = {f0 ◦w | w ∈f
W}. In
an apartment of type (A, f
W), we can define any notion defined in A and preserved by f
W. For
example, we can define faces, sectors, walls, etc.
Recall that an apartment of IN is a set of the form Ab = {[ηb,t] | t ∈R}. Let A be
an apartment of IN . We define Isom(A, A) as the set of h|A
A such that h ∈G is such that
h.A = A. As G acts transitively on the set of apartments (by Lemma 1.2.2), Isom(A, A) is

W (see Subsection 1.6), this equips A with the structure of an apartment of type (A, f
W). We
have the following theorem:
Theorem 1.9.1. The set IN is an affine building of type (A, f
W) in the sense of [Par00,
Définition 1.1]. In particular, it satisfies the following properties.
(A2) For every two apartments A and B, A ∩B is enclosed and there exists g ∈G such that
g.A = B and g fixes A ∩B.
(A3’) For every two faces F1, F2 of IN , there exists an apartment containing F1 ∪F2. This
applies in particular when F1 and F2 are points.
(A4) For every two sectors q, q′ of IN , there exists an apartment containing their germs at
infinity Q and Q′.
Proof. By [Par00, Théorème 1.21], the definition given here is equivalent to [Par00, Définition
1.1]. Then (A2) is implied by Proposition 1.6.2, using the transitivity of the action of G on
the set of apartments, (A3’) is Corollary 1.8.6 and (A4) is Corollary 1.8.4.
Let x, y ∈IN . Then by (A3’), there exists an apartment A containing x and y. We define
[x, y] as the segment joining x to y in A. By (A2), this segment does not depend on the choice
of A and is contained in any apartment containing x and y. If x, y ∈IN , we define
sx,y : [0, 1] →[x, y], t 7→(1 −t)x + ty.
(1.9.1)
Let A and A′ be two apartments such that A ∩A′ has non-empty interior.
By (A2),
there exists an apartment isomorphism f : A →A′. If f′ : A →A′ is an other apartment
isomorphism fixing A ∩A′, then f′−1 ◦f : A →A is an apartment isomorphism fixing A ∩A′.
In particular, it is an affine automorphism of A fixing a subset with non-empty interior and
thus it is the identity. Therefore f = f′: there exists a unique apartment isomorphism A →A′
fixing A ∩A′.

1.9.2
Exchange condition and sundial configuration
In this subsection, we introduce the exchange condition and the sundial configuration. These
two properties will be very useful in our study of masures.
The following proposition is called “exchange condition” in [BS14, 2] (see Figure 1.9.1).
Proposition 1.9.2. Let A and A′ be two distinct apartments of IN such that A ∩A′ is a
half-apartment. Let D1 = A′ \ A and D2 = A \ A′. Then A′′ := D1 ∪D2 is an apartment of
IN and A ∩A′ ∩A′′ = D1 ∩D2 is point, which is moreover a vertex of IN .
Proof. Using apartment isomorphisms, we can assume that A = A and by symmetry, we can
assume that A′ contains Q−∞. Let g ∈G be such that g.A = A′ and g fixes A ∩A′, which
exists by (A2). By Proposition 1.8.2, we can write g = x−α(a)t, with a ∈F and t ∈TO. Then
we have A′ = x−α(a).A. By (1.8.1), we have D1 = x−α(a).D+, where D+ = {y ∈A | y ≥
ω(a)} = D2. Let D−= {y ∈A | y ≤ω(a)}. By (1.8.2) and Lemma 1.2.1, we have:
x−α(a).D+ = xα(−a−1)(−α)∨(a)esxα(−a−1).D+ = xα(−a−1)(−α)∨(a)es.D+ = xα(−a−1).D−.
Therefore D1 ∪D2 = xα(−a−1).A. We set A′′ = xα(−a−1.A). Then A′′ is an apartment and
A ∩A′ ∩A′′ = {ω(a)}, which proves the result.

1.9.3
Retractions and Hecke paths
Retractions are an important tool in the study of buildings and masures. We introduce here
the retractions centered at infinity and deduce the Iwasawa decomposition of G. We then
study the image of a line segment of IN by a retraction.
Proposition 1.9.4. Let C be an alcove or an end of IN and A be an apartment containing
C. Then there exists a unique retraction ρ = ρC,A : IN →A such that for each apartment A′
containing C, ρ|A
A′ is the apartment isomorphism from A′ to A fixing A ∩A′.
Proof. Let x ∈IN . Then by (A3’) or Proposition 1.9.3, there exists an apartment Ax con-
taining C and x. Let f : Ax →A be the apartment isomorphism fixing A ∩Ax. Then if ρ
exists, we necessarily have ρ(x) = f(x). Now to ensure the existence of ρ it suffices to prove
that f(x) is independent of the choice of Ax. Let A′ be an apartment containing x and C
and let f′ : A′ →A be the apartment isomorphism fixing A ∩A′. Let ϕ : A′ →Ax be the
apartment isomorphism fixing A′ ∩Ax. Then f′, f ◦ϕ : A′ →A are apartment isomorphism
fixing C, and thus they fix a subset of A′ with non-empty interior. Therefore f ◦ϕ = f′. In
particular, f′(x) = f ◦ϕ(x) = f(x), which proves that ρ is well-defined.
Notation 1.9.5. Recall that Q+∞(resp. Q−∞) is the end of A consisting of the subsets of
A containing a neighborhood of +∞(resp. −∞). We denote by ρ+∞and ρ−∞the retractions

If C is an alcove of A, we say that C is positive if it is of the form [x, x+[ for some x ∈A
and negative otherwise.
We now study the image of a segment-line by a retraction. We prove that this image,
which is called a Hecke path, satisfies very restrictive properties (for example they can fold
only once). Hecke paths are crucial in our study of masures: we use it to prove that the
intersection of two apartments is convex. These paths are also used in many applications of
the theory of masures to the study of Kac–Moody groups.

1.10
Distance d on IN
We now introduce a distance d on IN . We introduce two equivalent definitions of d and use
it to describe the G-orbits of IL .
1.10.0.1
Definition of the distance
We define a map d : IN × IN →R≥0 as follows. Let x, y ∈IN . By Corollary 1.8.6, there
exists an apartment A containing x and y. Let g ∈G be such that g.A = A ≃R. We set
d(x, y) = |g−1.x −g−1.y|. This does not depend on the choices of A and g. Indeed, take
an apartment A′ and g′ ∈G such that g′.A′ = A. Let h ∈G be such that h.A = A′ and
h fixes A ∩A′. Then n := gh−1g′−1 stabilizes A so it belongs to N. Let w ∈f
W be the
element induced by n on A. For z ∈A ∩A′, we have g.z = ng′h.z = ng′.z = w.g′.z and thus
|g′.y −g′.x| = |w−1(g.y −g.x)| = |g.y −g.x|, since f
W acts by isometry on A. This proves that
d is well-defined.
By definition, d is invariant under the action of G.
Actually, since StabGL2(F)(A) =
N · TGL2(F) acts by isometry on A, d is also GL2(F)-invariant. It is obviously symmetric and
its restriction to every apartment is isometric to (R, |·|). It also satisfies the triangle inequality
and thus it is a distance, but this requires a bit more work, see Corollary 1.10.4.
Proposition 1.10.1. If C is an alcove or an end of A and A is an apartment containing
C, then ρ = ρA,C : (IN , d) →(A, d) is 1-Lipschitz continuous. In particular, IN satisfies
axiom (A5’) (see [Par00] or [Rou23, 2.4.4.1 Definition]): for all apartment A and for all
x ∈IN , there exists a retraction ρ : IN →A such that ρ−1({x}) = {x}, which is 1-Lipschitz
continuous in restriction to any apartment.
Proof. Using an element of G, we may assume that A = A. Then the fact that ρ is 1-Lipschitz
continuous follows from Proposition 1.9.8. Let us prove axiom (A5’). Let x ∈IN and A
be an apartment containing x. Let C be an alcove of A dominating x and ρ = ρA,C. Let
y ∈ρ−1({x}) and A′ be an apartment containing C and y. Then the restriction f of ρ to A′ is
the apartment isomorphism fixing A ∩A′. Therefore f(y) = f(x) = x and thus x = y, which
proves that ρ−1({x}) = {x}. We deduce that (A5’) is satisfied.
Proposition 1.10.2. Let A be an apartment of IN and z ∈IN . Then:
1. there exists a unique projA(z) ∈A such that d(z, A) = d(z, projA(z)).
2. For all y ∈A, we have [y, z] = [y, projA(z)]⊔]projA(z), z] and d(y, z) = d(y, projA(z)) +
d(projA(z), z).

Corollary 1.10.4. The map d : IN →IN is a distance.
Proof. It remains to prove that d satisfies the triangle inequality. Let x, y, z ∈IN . Using
apartment isomorphisms, we can assume that x, y ∈A ≃R and that x ≤y, when we identify
A with R. Let a = projA(z). There are three cases: a ∈] −∞, x], a ∈]x, y[ and a ∈[y, +∞[.
If a ∈] −∞, x], then d(x, y) + d(y, z) = d(x, y) + d(x, y) + d(a, x) + d(z, a) = d(x, z) +
2d(x, y) ≥d(x, z).
If a ∈]x, y[, then d(x, z)+d(z, y) = d(x, a)+d(a, z)+d(a, y)+d(a, z) = d(x, z)+2d(a, y) ≥
d(x, z).
If a ∈[y, +∞[, then d(x, z) + d(z, y) = d(x, y) + d(y, a) + d(a, z) = d(x, z). In the three
cases, we have the triangle inequality, which proves that d is a distance.
Corollary 1.10.5. For every x, y ∈IN , we have
[x, y] = {z ∈IN | d(x, z) + d(z, y) = d(x, y)}.

which implies z ∈A and z ∈[x, y], which proves the other inclusion.

1.11
Tree structures on IN and IL
In this section, we prove that IN and IL , equipped with their distance, are trees. Recall that
Λ = ω(F×).
Definition 1.11.1. A Λ-metric space is a metric space (X, d) where X is a set and d is a
distance on X with values in Λ ∩R≥0. Let X be a set and d : X →Λ ∩R+ be a distance. A
Λ-segment of X is a subset of X of the form α([a, b] ∩Λ), where a, b ∈Λ and α : [a, b] →X
is an isometry. The endpoints of the segment are α(a) and α(b) and we say that the segment
joints α(a) and α(b).
A Λ-tree is a Λ-metric space (X, d) such that:
1. (X, d) is geodesic, i.e for every x, y ∈X, there exists a segment in X with endpoints x
and y,
2. if two segments of X intersect in a single point, which is an endpoint of both, then their
union is a segment of X.
3. the intersection of two segments with a common endpoint is also a segment.
Note that the Z-trees are the graphs which are connected and without circuit, by [Chi01,
2 Example 2].
For x, y ∈IL , we denote by [x, y]L the segment of IL between x and y, i.e [x, y]L =
[x, y] ∩IL . If x, y ∈Λ ⊂R, we set [x, y]Λ = [x, y] ∩Λ.
Theorem 1.11.2. (see [Ser77, Chapitre 2, 1.1 Théorème 1] and [Chi01, Chapter 4, Theorem
3.4])
1. The metric space (IN , d) is an R-tree.
2. The metric space (IL , d) is a Λ-tree, where Λ = ω(F×).
Proof. (1) By definition of d, if x, y ∈IN and if g ∈G is such that g.x, g.y ∈A, then [x, y] is
isometric to [g.x, g.y], which proves that (IN , d) is geodesic. Moreover if x, y ∈IL , then by
Lemma 1.3.3, g.[x, y]L = [g.x, g.y]L ≃[g.x, g.y]Λ. Thus IL is a geodesic Λ-metric space in
the sense of [Chi01, Chapter 1; 2].
Let J1, J2 be two segments of IN , which intersect in a single point, which is an end point
of both.
We can write J1 = [x, y] and J2 = [x, z], with x, y, z ∈IN .
Using apartment
isomorphisms, we can assume that [x, y] ⊂A. If z ∈A, then [z, x] ∪[x, y] is the segment
[z, y]. Assume now z /∈A. Let a = projA(z), with the notation of Proposition 1.10.2. Then
by Proposition 1.10.2, we have [z, y] = [z, a]⊔]a, y] and [z, x] = [z, a]∪]a, x]. Identify A and R.
Up to changing the identification, we can assume that x ≥a and that x ≤y, if a = x. As

[x, y] = [z, x] ∪[x, y], which proves that J1 ∪J2 is a segment of IN .
Assume moreover x, y, z ∈IL . As J1 ∪J2 is a segment, it is contained in an apartment
of IN .
Thus we can assume that x, y, z ∈A and then [z, y]L = [z, x]L ∪[x, y]L .

A be an apartment containing the , then a ∈IL , by Proposition 1.10.2. Then [z, y]L =
[z, a]L ∪[a, x]L ∪[x, y]L = [z, x]L ∪[x, y]L is a segment of IL .
Let now J1, J2 be two segments with a common endpoint. Write J1 = [x, y] and J2 = [x, z],
with x, y, z ∈IN . Let A be an apartment containing x and y. Set a = projA(z). Then
[x, y] ∩[x, z] ⊂[x, z] ∩A = [x, a]. Therefore [x, y] ∩[x, z] = [x, y] ∩[x, a]. As x, y and a belong

If x, y and z are contained in an apartment AL , then [x, y]L ∩[x, z]L is a segment of this
apartment and thus it is a segment of IL . If x, y, z are not contained in any apartment,
then a ∈IL , by Proposition 1.10.2 and thus [x, y]L ∩[x, z]L = [x, a]L . This proves the
theorem.
Note that when Λ = Z, then IL has no leaf (or terminal vertex) since AL has no leaf. In
this case, IN can be obtained from IL by attaching an edge to each adjacent vertices, (where
two vertices x, y are adjacent if d(x, y) = 1). For x ∈IN , we denote by Cx the set of alcoves
dominating x.

Parohoric viewpoint on the tree
We defined the tree of SL2(F) as a quotient of a set of ultrametric norms on F2. This approach
has several advantages: it is explicit, relatively elementary, it enables to make computations,
etc. However, we do not know if this approach enables to define the Bruhat–Tits building of

For arbitrary split reductive groups and Kac–Moody groups, Bruhat–Tits buildings and

in the special case of SL2 and relate it to the construction we made.

## Page 38


Chapter 2
Kac–Moody algebras and vectorial
apartment of a masure
The aim of this paper is to study Kac–Moody groups over valued fields. There are several
possible definitions of Kac–Moody groups, but for all of them, a Kac–Moody group is supposed
to “integrate” a Kac–Moody algebra, in some sense. In this chapter, we recall some facts on
Kac–Moody algebras.
Since the works of Cartan and Killing at the end of the nineteenth century, the classification
of finite dimensional semi-simple Lie algebras over C is known. One way to describe it is to use
Cartan matrices: to each such Lie algebra, one associates a Cartan matrix, which is a matrix
whose coefficients are integers and satisfy some conditions. Serre used Cartan matrices to give
a presentation of such Lie algebras by generators and relations (see [Ser66, Chapitre 6]).
In 1967, Kac and Moody independently introduced a new class of Lie algebras, the Kac–
Moody algebras (or more precisely, the Kac–Moody Lie algebras). A Kac–Moody algebra is
a Lie algebra defined by Serre’s presentation, but for a Kac–Moody matrix (often called a
generalized Cartan matrix) instead of a Cartan matrix.
Kac–Moody algebras are infinite dimensional, unless they are associated with a Cartan
matrix. Despite this difference, Kac–Moody algebras share many properties with finite di-
mensional semi-simple Lie algebras, which we now sketch. A Kac–Moody algebra g comes
together with a Cartan Lie subalgebra h, which is a finite dimensional commutative Lie alge-
bra. Then g decomposes as a direct sum of its eigenspaces with respect to h: g = h⊕L
α∈∆gα,
where ∆is the set of α ∈h∗\ {0} whose associated eigenspace gα is non-zero. One can also
define a vectorial Weyl group W v, which is infinite in general, which acts on h∗and stabilizes
∆. The elements of ∆which are the transform of a simple roots by an element of W v are
called real roots and the other roots are called imaginary. Then g is finite dimensional if and
only if all the roots are real.
In this chapter, we introduce briefly Kac–Moody algebras and their basic properties. We
also introduce the vectorial apartment of a masure. It is a finite dimensional vector space -
more precisely, it is an R form of the Cartan subalgebra of g - equipped with the hyperplane
arrangement defined by the real roots of (g, h).
This chapter is organized as follows. In Section 2.1, we describe the Kac–Moody algebra
sln(C). In Section 2.2, we define the Kac–Moody algebras. In Section 2.3, we introduce the
vectorial apartment associated with the Kac–Moody algebra. In Section 2.4, we describe the
vectorial apartment in examples: in the affine case and in the indefinite case for particular
choices of size 2 Kac–Moody matrices.

Kac–Moody algebras
In this section, we define Kac–Moody algebras by generators and relations. We first Kac–
Moody data, which are the data to which Kac–Moody algebras and groups are associated.

2.2.1

In this subsection, we define Kac–Moody data.

2.2.1.1
Kac–Moody matrices
Definition 2.2.1. A Kac–Moody matrix (also known as a generalized Cartan matrix) is a
square matrix A = (ai,j)i,j∈I with integers coefficients, indexed by a finite set I and satisfying:
1. ∀i ∈I, ai,i = 2
2. ∀(i, j) ∈I2 | i ̸= j, ai,j ≤0
3. ∀(i, j) ∈I2, ai,j = 0 ⇔aj,i = 0.
The matrix A is said to be decomposable if for some reordering of the indices, one can
write A as a non trivial block diagonal matrix

if it is not decomposable.

i )i∈I) is free.

2.3
Vectorial apartment of a Kac–Moody algebra
The masure is a union of subsets called apartments, which are all isomorphic to one of them
called the standard apartment A. As a set, A is Y ⊗R, where Y is the cocharacter lattice
of S. It is then equipped with additionnal structures: the structure of a vectorial apartment,
which we describe now and the structure of an affine apartment, which will be described in
Subsection 4.1.1.
In a first approximation, the affine apartment is A, equipped with the hyperplane ar-
rangement H0 := {α−1({0}) | α ∈Φ}, whereas the affine apartment is (A, HΛ), where
HΛ := {α−1({k}) | k ∈Λ, α ∈Φ}, where Φ ⊂∆is the set of real roots defined in Sub-
section 2.3.1 and Λ = ω(F×), when we study a Kac–Moody group over a valued field (F, ω).
Note that the vectorial apartment does not take into account the valuation on the field over
which we study the Kac–Moody group.
The apartment A is naturally equipped with a Weyl group W v, which is generated by
reflections with respect to the elements of H0. The set of simple roots (αi)i∈I enables to
define a fundamental chamber Cv
f, which enables to define the Tits cone T = W v.Cv
f and
its opposite −T . The Tits cone naturally decomposes into vectorial faces, whose maximal
ones are called vectorial chambers. The face decomposition of the Tits cone enables to define
the “face structure at infinity” of the masures: it enables to define sector-germs and, more
generally, sector-face-germs.
In the reductive case, T = A = −T . In the (non-reductive indecomposable) Kac–Moody
case however, T is a strict cone of A and −Cv
f ∩T is almost reduced to zero in general. It
corresponds to the fact that in the reductive case, there is only one conjugacy class of Borel
subgroups whereas in the Kac–Moody case, there are two.
We start by giving the definitions and then we describe the vectorial apartment when the
Kac–Moody matrix has size 2.

2.4
Vectorial apartments in the non-reductive case
In this section, we describe the vectorial apartment in the affine case and the indefinite case.
In 2.4.1, we describe the link between Kac–Moody matrices associated to untwisted affine
Kac–Moody algebras and loop Lie algebras. We then describe the vectorial apartment of such
algebras by using the affine apartment of the underlying semi-simple finite dimensional matrix.
In 2.4.2, we describe the vectorial apartment in the indefinite case. We give a more precise
description in the two dimensional case.

Chapter 3
Split Kac–Moody groups, à la Tits
and à la Mathieu
Let S be a free Kac–Moody data. In Chapter 2, we introduced a Kac–Moody algebra to S.
In [Tit87], Tits defined the constructive Tits functor associated to this data. It is a group
functor GS from the category of rings to the category of groups, which integrates g in some
sense. Moreover, Tits also defined a series of axioms that a group functor needs to satisfy to
be called a “minimal Kac–Moody group functor” - minimal meaning that it integrates only
the real roots of (g, h). He proved that GS satisfies its axioms over the fields and that if K,
the value of a functor satisfying its axioms is unique and isomorphic to GS(K). If R is a ring,
then GS(R) is generated by a torus TS(R), isomorphic to (R×)dim h, and by the root groups
xα(R), α ∈Φ, which are all isomorphic to (R, +).
On the other hand, Mathieu defined in [Mat89] a completed Kac–Moody group functor
Gma+
S
= Gma+ from the category of rings to the category of groups. It integrates the real
roots and the positive imaginary roots of (g, h) and there is a group functor morphism from
GS to Gma+, which is an injection on every field. It is used by Marquis to define a “minimal
group functor” which satisfies Tits’s axioms (on all rings and not only on fields).
Our objective is to study minimal Kac–Moody groups. However, certain commutation
relations are missing in them, compared to the reductive case. More precisely, assume that g
is finite dimensional and that K is a field. Then by [Ste16, Lemma 15], for all α, β ∈∆= Φ
such that α ̸= −β, the commutator [xα(K), xβ(K)] is contained in Q
γ∈(Z≥1α+Z≥1β)∩Φ xγ(K).
Consider now the Kac–Moody group G = SL2(K[t, t−1]), where K is a field. Its root system
is {±˚α + kδ | k ∈Z}, for the notation of Subsection 2.4.1. Then by [Mar18, Example 7.85 &

In order to handle this kind of elements, it is convenient to embed G in SL2(K((t))) or
SL2(K((t−1))).
More generally, if G is a general Kac–Moody group, we use its Mathieu’s
completions Gma+ and Gma−(where p and n stand for positive and negative), which are
SL2(K((t))) and SL2(K((t−1))) in the case of SL2(K[t, t−1]).
The definition of Gma+ is complicated over arbitrary rings. However, over fields, a simpler
description, by generators and relations was provided by Marquis in [Mar18, 8.5]. Let U+ =
⟨xα | α ∈Φ+⟩and U−= ⟨xα | α ∈Φ−⟩. The group functor U+ is completed in a group
functor Uma+ and then if K is a field, Gma+(K) is defined as a quotient of the free product
G(K) ∗Uma+(K) by certain relations (see Definition 3.3.1).
An advantage of Uma+ compared to U+ is that we can use the “coordinates” Xα, α ∈∆+
introduced by Rousseau in [Rou16] to describe its elements (Rousseau does not define the Xα
48


## Page 51


there, but he defines the “twisted exponentials”, from which the Xα are defined). The Xα
generalize the xα used in the reductive case. Let R be a ring. For α ∈∆+, we have a map
Xα : gα,Z ⊗R →Uma+(R) (depending on many choices). Then if we fix an order on ∆+
compatible with the height, we can express every element of Uma+(R) uniquely as a product
Q
α∈∆+ Xα(uα), where (uα) ∈Q
α∈∆+ gα,Z ⊗R. These coordinates are probably useful in
the study of Kac–Moody groups over arbitrary rings or fields in general. For our purpose,
where R = F is a valued field and G = GS(F), they enable to define the fixator U+ and
U−-components of the fixator of a subset of A, for the action of G on the masure.
In Section 3.1, we define the constructive Tits functor associated to a Kac–Moody data.
Its values on any field K is the “minimal Kac–Moody group over K“.
In Section 3.2, we define and study Uma+, which is a completion of U+, and which is a
standard unipotent subgroup of Mathieu’s completed group.
In Section 3.3, we introduce Marquis’s scheme theoretic and Mathieu’s completion of the
minimal Kac–Moody group.
3.1
Minimal Kac–Moody group
In this section, we introduce and study the constructive Tits functor GS associated by Tits
with each Kac–Moody data S. It is defined by generators and relations. For a field K, GS(K)
is what we call the minimal split Kac–Mody group over K associated with S. For an arbitrary
ring R, the “right” definition of the minimal Kac–Moody group over R associated with S is
not GS(R). We give Marquis’s definition in Subsection 3.3.3, using Mathieu’s completion.
3.1.1
Nilpotent sets and prenilpotent pairs of roots
In this subsection, we define the notion of nilpotency for sets of roots. This notion is important
to define the constructive Tits functor: it is used to define certain commutation relations
satisfied by this group.
Definition 3.1.1. Let Ψ ⊂∆+. We say that Ψ is closed (resp. strongly closed) if for every
α, β ∈Ψ such that α + β ∈∆+ (resp. for all p, q ∈Z≥1 such that pα + qβ ∈∆), we have
α + β ∈Ψ (resp. pα + qβ ∈Ψ).
Let Ψ′ ⊂Ψ ⊂∆+ be closed subset of roots.
We say that Ψ′ is coclosed in Ψ (resp.
strongly coclosed) if Ψ \ Ψ′ is closed (resp. if Ψ is strongly closed and Ψ \ Ψ′ is strongly
closed).
We also call Ψ′ an ideal in Ψ (resp. a strong ideal in Ψ) if α+β ∈Ψ′ (resp. pα+qβ ∈
Ψ′) whenever α ∈Ψ, β ∈Ψ′ and α + β ∈Ψ (resp.
p, q ∈Z≥1, α ∈Ψ, β ∈Ψ′ and
pα + qβ ∈∆+).
Rousseau and Marquis use different definitions of closeness and ideals in [Rou16] and
[Mar18]. We add the adjective “strong” to refer to the notion used by Rousseau. We do not
know whether it is really different from the one of Marquis.
Definition 3.1.2. Let Ψ be a subset of Φ (or ∆). The set Ψ is said to be prenilpotent
if there exists w, w′ ∈W v such that w.Ψ ⊂∆+ and w′.Ψ ⊂∆−. Then Ψ is finite since
w.Ψ ⊂Inv(w′w−1). We say that Ψ is nilpotent if it is prenilpotent and closed. A set Ψ is
prenilpotent if and only if −Ψ is prenilpotent.
If {α, β} ⊂Φ is a prenilpotent pair, we denote by ]α, β[N the finite set (Z>0α + Z>0β) ∩Φ
and we set [α, β]N =]α, β[N∪{α, β}. For all α ∈Φ, the pair {α, −α} is not prenilpotent.
In the remaining part of this subsection, we give criteria for pairs of roots to be prenilpotent
in order to illustrate this notion.

3.2
The affine group scheme Uma+
We now introduce the “affine group scheme“ Uma+. It is a “completion” of the functor U+. If
R is a ring, then Uma+(R) is defined as a subgroup of a completion of the Hopf enveloping
algebra UR.
We start by defining the completion of a graded vector space (Subsection 3.2.1) and apply
this to define the completed envelopping algebra bU+
R.
We define the twisted exponentials
(Subsection 3.2.3), which enable to give a normal form to the elements of Uma+(R). We study
decompositions of Uma+ (see Subsections 3.2.4 and 3.2.5). We then describe explicitly Uma+
in the case of affine SL2, using Garland-Mitzman polynomials (see Subsection 3.2.6).

3.2.3
The group scheme Uma+ and twisted exponentials
We now introduce the group-scheme Uma+. We then define the “twisted exponentials“, which
enable to describe the elements of Uma+.
Definition/Proposition 3.2.1. We call an element x of c
U+
R\{0} group-like if ∇(x) = x⊗x.
In this case, x is invertible, x−1 = τ(x) and ϵ(x) = 1. Moreover, the set of group-like elements
of c
U+
R forms a subgroup of ( c
U+
R)×.
Proof. Let x ∈c
U+
R be such that ∇(x) = x⊗x. Then by definition of a Hopf algebra (see the two
diagrams preceding [Mar18, Definition A.12]), we have (ϵ⊗Id)◦∇(x) = 1⊗x = (ϵ⊗Id)(x⊗x)
and hence ϵ(x) = 1. We also have τ(x)x = ϵ(x) = 1 and xτ(x) = ϵ(x) = 1, which proves
that x is invertible. Moreover, as ∇is an algebra morphism, we have ∇(x−1) = ∇(x)−1 =
x−1 ⊗x−1.

3.3
Completed Kac–Moody groups
Now that we defined the constructive Tits functor G and the affine group scheme Uma+, we
can define completions of G: the scheme theoretic completion Gsch+ and Mathieu’s comple-
tion Gma+. The values of these functors are isomorphic over fields. We then use Mathieu’s
completed group to define a minimal Tits functor.
3.3.1
Marquis’s scheme theoretic completion
In [Mar18, 8.5.4], Marquis introduce the (positive) scheme theoretic completion Gsch+ of G.
Its is defined by generators and relations. For every field K, Gsch+(K) is isomorphic, as a
topological group, to Gma+(K) ([Mar18, Proposition 8.120]). The “advantage” of Gsch+ is that
its definition is simpler than the one of Gma+.
Let R be a ring. Let i ∈I. Then by [Mar18, (8.55)], we have two representations by con-
tinuous automorphisms AdR : U−αi(R) →Aut(U∆+\{αi}(R)) and AdR : T →Aut(Uma+(R)).

3.3.3
Minimal Kac–Moody groups over rings
3.3.3.1
Axiomatic properties of Tits functor
Let S be a Kac–Moody root data. In Subsection 3.1.4, we introduced the constructive Tits
functor GS. In some sense, it integrates the Kac–Moody algebra gS. In order to give a precise
meaning to “integrating gS”, Tits defines a series of fives axioms ((KM1) to (KMG5)) that a
group functor has to satisfy to be a “minimal Kac–Moody group functor of type S” (see [Tit87]
and also [Rém02, 8]). Tits claims that if G is a group functor satisfying its axioms, then there
exists a morphism of group functors π : GS →G satisfying certain conditions and that under
some mild conditions, π is an isomorphism for each field (see [Tit87, Theorem 1]). However he
only sketches the proofs and we do not know a reference where it is proved in details. Marquis
proposes in [Mar18, 7.5] a slight modification of Tits’s axioms: he defines axioms (KMG1’),
(KMG2’), (KMG4’) and (KMG5’), which are close to Tits’s axioms. We call such a functor
a Tits functor. He gives a detailed proof of [Tit87, Theorem 1] in certain cases (when S is
adjoint). He also proves the existence of a Tits functor ([Mar18, 8.8]) Gmin
S
= Gmin. Over
arbitrary rings, its definition use Mathieu’s group and we recall it here. This functor has the
properties (Gmin1) to (Gmin4) and (KMG5) below (see [MM25, 2.6]).

3.3.3.2
Definition of a Tits functor
Let i ∈I. We denote by UX

This is the minimal group functor of type S.
This group functor is introduced by Marquis in [Mar18, Definition 8.126]. By [Mar18,
Proposition 8.129], it is a nondegenerate Tits functor in the sense of [Mar18, Definition 7.83]

## Page 71


Chapter 4
Abstract masure: a convexity result
In Chapter 6, we will define the masure associated with a Kac–Moody group G, as the quotient
I = (G×A)/ ∼, where A is the apartment associated with G and ∼is an equivalence relation.
This definition is complicated, but we can recover many properties of I by considering it as an
abstract masure. Roughly speaking (see Definition 4.1.6 for a precise definition), this means
that I is a union of subsets of I called apartments satisfying the following properties:
(MA1) each apartment is “isomorphic” to the standard apartment,
(MA2) for every two apartments A, B of I, A ∩B is a finite intersection of half-spaces of A,
delimited by real roots of G, and there exists an apartment isomorphism from A to B
fixing A ∩B,
(MA3) some pairs of filters V1, V2 are contained in a common apartment.
Note that when I is associated with a split Kac–Moody group G over a valued field with
split maximal torus T, the standard apartment is Y ⊗R equipped with some set of walls,
where Y is the cocharacter lattice. The apartments are then the g.A such that g ∈G.
While in the reductive case, axiom (MA2) is rather easy to obtain, it is harder to prove in
the Kac–Moody case. The main difficulties are the fact that the Tits cone is a proper subcone
of A and the fact that the construction of I involves completions of G, which have imaginary
roots. Actually in [Rou11] and [Rou16], Rousseau proves that I satisfies weak versions of
(MA2). We then obtained that these weak versions actually imply (MA2) in [Héb20] and
[Héb22].
In this chapter, we define abstract masures. We then prove that a certain series of axioms
imply (MA2) (see Theorem 4.9.6). We work abstractly, which means that we consider a set
covered by apartments and satisfying certain axioms, but we do not assume that this set is
attached to a Kac–Moody group.
While a part of this chapter is purely building theoretic (from Subsection 4.8), we introduce
objects which are particularly important in the theory of Kac–Moody groups over valued fields,
namely retractions and Hecke paths.

Lemma 4.1.3. Let w ∈W a be such that w fixes a wall M of A. Then w ∈{Id, rM}.
Proof. We write w = τ ◦w, with τ a translation of A and w ∈W v. Then w.M is a wall parallel
to M. Let M0 be the wall parallel to M containing 0. Then w.M0 is a wall parallel to M0 and
containing 0. Therefore w.M0 = M0. Let Cv be a vectorial chamber dominating a vector panel
of M0. Then w.Cv is a chamber adjacent to Cv. Consequently, w.Cv ∈{Cv, rM0.Cv}, where
rM0 is the reflection of W v with respect to M0. As the action of W v on the set of vectorial
chambers is simply transitive, we deduce that w ∈{Id, rM0}, which proves the lemma.

In the three situations, we say that Q and Q′ are adjacent if d(Q, Q′) ∈{1}∪{ri | i ∈I}.

means that for all x ∈A, x + Q and x + Q′ dominate a common sector-face panel, i.e a sector
face of codimension 1.
A gallery Γ = (Q1, . . . , Qk) is a finite sequence of either alcoves, sector-germs or vectorial
chambers (all the Qi have to be of the same type) such that for each i ∈J1, k −1K, Qi and
Qi+1 are adjacent or equal. The length of Γ is k −1.

a gallery between Q and Q′.
Moreover, the minimal possible length of such a gallery is
ℓ(d(Q, Q′)).

4.1.1.5
Apartment of type A
An apartment of type A is a set A equipped with a nonempty set Isom(A, A) of bijec-
tions (called Weyl-isomorphisms) such that if f0 ∈Isom(A, A) then f ∈Isom(A, A) if and
only if there exists w ∈W a satisfying f = f0 ◦w. We say isomorphism instead of Weyl-
isomorphism in the sequel. Let A and A′ be two apartments of type A. An (apartment)
isomorphism between A and A′ is an element of the form g ◦f−1, where f ∈Isom(A, A) and
g ∈Isom(A, A′). We extend all the notions that are preserved by W a to each apartment.
Thus sectors, enclosures, faces and chimneys are well-defined in any apartment of type A.
4.1.2
Weak masures and masures
In this subsection, we introduce several axioms of masures. We then give the definition of
masures.
4.1.2.1
Axioms
We consider a space I endowed with a covering A of subsets called apartments such that:
(MA1) Any A ∈A is equipped with the structure of an apartment of type A.
73


## Page 76


The elements of A are called apartments.
We can define the notion of walls, faces,
enclosure clA, ∆-enclosure cl∆
A, segments [x, y]A between two points x, y of A, ...
in any
apartment A of type A. We also consider the following axioms:
(MA2) Let A and B be two apartments. Then A ∩B is enclosed, i.e there exist k ∈N and half-
apartments D1, . . . , Dk of A such that A ∩B = Tk
i=1 Di and there exists an apartment
isomorphism ϕ : A →B fixing A ∩B.
(MA3) Let Q be a splayed chimney germ and R be a local face or a chimney germ. Then there
exists an apartment containing Q and R.
(wMA3) (“weak MA3”) : Let Q, Q′ be two sector-germs at infinity. Then there exists an apartment
containing them.
(OCO) (“ordered convexity”): Let V be a filter on an apartment A such that there exists a
generic sector-face f based at some x ∈A such that {x} ⊂V ⊂f (i.e f ∈V and x ∈Ω,
for all Ω∈V). Then for every apartment A′ containing V, cl∆
A(V) ⊂A′ and there exists
an apartment isomorphism from A to A′ fixing cl∆
A(V).
In an space satisfying (MA1) and (wMA3), we say that two sector-germs at infinity are
adjacent if there exists an apartment containing them in which they are adjacent sector-germs.
We now introduce a last axiom:
(SC) (“sundial configuration”) Let Q and Q′ be two adjacent sector-germs at infinity and A
be an apartment containing Q. Then there exist two opposite half-apartments D1 and
D2 of A (i.e D1 ∩D2 is the wall of both D1 and D2) such that for both i ∈{1, 2}, there
exists an apartment containing Di ∪Q′.
Note that axiom (OCO) is a strong version of axiom (MAO) of [Rou11].
It is clearly
implied by (MA2).
Axiom (wMA3) is a weak version of axiom (MA3), since sector-germs at infinity are chim-
ney germs. These two axioms are building theoretic translations of decompositions of G, when
I is associated to a Kac–Moody group G. Axiom (wMA3) is the translation of the Bruhat
and the Birkhoff decompositions in G (see (3.1.21) and (3.1.22)) and (MA3) is a consequence
of a decomposition of G proved in Subsection 6.7.4.
Definition 4.1.6. Let S be a free Kac–Moody data. Let A = (S, (Λα)α∈Φ) be an apartment
in the sense of Definition 4.1.2.
1. A weak masure of type A is a set I endowed with a covering A of subsets called
apartments satisfying (MA1), (wMA3), (OCO) and (SC).
2. A masure of type A is a set I endowed with a covering A of subsets called apartments
satisfying (MA1), (MA2) and (MA3).
The main theorem of this chapter is Theoreme 4.9.6, which asserts that a weak masure
satisfies (MA2). Along the proof of Theorem 4.9.6, we obtain properties of masures which are
useful for the applications of the theory of masures. From Section 4.2 to Section 4.5, we study
basic properties of (weak) masures. In Section 4.6, we introduce and study Hecke paths, which
are a fundamental tool in the study of Kac–Moody groups, for example they enable to define
Hecke algebras of Kac–Moody groups ([GR14] and [BGR16]). Sections 4.8 and 4.9 might be
less useful for the reader ready to admit that I satisfies (MA2).

4.1.3
Apartment isomorphisms fixing filters
We equip A with its topology of finite dimensional affine space. As the elements of W a are
homeomorphisms for this topology, this equips any apartment A of type A with a topology.
Remark 4.1.7. Let A, B be two apartments and f, f′ : A →B be apartment isomorphisms.
Assume that f and f′ coincide on a subset of A ∩B with non-empty interior. Then f = f′.
Indeed, identify A and A. Then f′−1 ◦f : A →A is an apartment isomorphism and thus it
belongs to W a. Therefore f′−1 ◦f is an affine automorphism fixing an open subset of A and
thus f′−1 ◦f is the identity.
Lemma 4.1.8. Let (I, A) be a space satisfying (MA1) and (OCO). Let A, B be two apartments
containing a sector q. Then there exists a unique apartment isomorphism f : A →B fixing
A ∩B. Moreover, if y ∈A ∩B, then the translate of q at y (in A) is contained in A ∩B.
Proof. We identify A and A. Let q be a sector contained in A ∩B. Write q = x + Cv, with
Cv a vectorial chamber of A. By (OCO), there exists an apartment isomorphism f : A →B
fixing q. Let y ∈A ∩B. Then (y + Cv) ∩(x + Cv) is a sector of A and we can write it z + Cv,
for some z ∈A. Let Ω= y ∪(z + Cv) ⊂y + Cv. Then by (OCO), y + Cv ⊂cl∆(Ω) ⊂A ∩B
and there exists an apartment isomorphism fy : A →B fixing y + Cv. But fy and f coincide
on z + Cv and thus by Remark 4.1.7, f = fy and f(y) = fy(y) = y. Therefore f fixes A ∩B,
which proves the lemma.
4.2
Retractions centered at infinity and inseparable filters
In this section, we prove that an inseparable filter (see Definition 4.2.1 below) and a sector-germ
at infinity are always contained in a common apartment. This enables to define retractions
centered at infinity, since points are particular cases of inseparable filters.
Recall that if α ∈Φ and x ∈R, D(α, x) = {a ∈A | α(a) + x ≥0}.
Definition 4.2.1. Let V be a filter on A. We say that V is inseparable if for every α ∈Φ
and x ∈Λα, we have V ⊂D(α, x) or V ⊂D(−α, −x).
Example 4.2.2.
1. A face or a sector-face-germ at infinity is inseparable.
2. The germ at infinity of a ray is inseparable.
3. A chimney germ is inseparable.
4. Let V be an inseparable filter. Then cl(V) is inseparable.
Let Q, Q′ be two sector-germs of A of the same sign ϵ. We say that they are adjacent if
for some apartment A containing Q and Q′ (which exists by (wMA3)) and some apartment
isomorphism f : A →A, f(Q) and f(Q′) are adjacent. This does not depend on the choice of
f and by Lemma 4.1.8, this does not depend on the choice of A. This enables us to define the
notion of galleries between two sector-germs of the same sign, similarly as in (4.1.1.4).
Proposition 4.2.3. Let Q be a sector-germ at infinity and V be an inseparable filter. Then
there exists an apartment containing Q and V.
Proof. Let A be an apartment containing V. Let R be a sector-germ of A of the same sign
as Q. Let B be an apartment containing Q and R. Let Γ = (Q1, . . . , Qn) be a gallery of
sector-germs from R = Q1 to Q = Qn (in B). By the sundial configuration (SC), we can
write A = D1 ∪D2, with Di ∪Q2 contained in some apartment Ai, for both i ∈{1, 2}. As
V is inseparable, either D1 or D2 contains V. Up to exchanging D1 and D2, we may assume
D1 ⊃V. Then we can replace Γ by (Q2, . . . , Qn) and by induction, we deduce the result.
75


## Page 78


Corollary 4.2.4. Let F be a face (resp. s : [0, 0+[→I be a half-segment germ) and Q be a
sector-germ. Then there exists an apartment containing F and Q (resp. s and Q).
Corollary 4.2.5. Let F1, F2 be two faces of I based at the same point. Then there exists an
apartment containing F1 and F2.
Proof. Let C1 be an alcove dominating F1. By (OCO), any apartment containing C1 contains
F1. Therefore we can assume that F1 = C1. Let A be an apartment containing C1 and q
be the sector based at the origin x of C1 and containing C1. Let Q be the sector-germ at
infinity of q. By Proposition 4.2.3, there exists an apartment A′ containing C2 and Q. Then
A contains x and Q and thus by (OCO), A ∩A′ contains q and hence C1, which proves the
corollary.
Definition 4.2.6. Let x ∈I and Q be a sector-germ at infinity. Then by Proposition 4.2.3
there exists an apartment A containing x and Q. We denote by x + Q the sector of A based
at x and whose germ at infinity is Q. This sector is well-defined, independently of the choice
of A, by (OCO).
Let Q be a sector-germ at infinity and A, B be two apartments containing Q, then we
have
y + Q ⊂A ∩B, ∀y ∈A ∩B,

by Lemma 4.1.8.

We set ρA,Q(x) = f(x).

independently on the choice of Ax. Indeed, if A′ is an other apartment containing x and Q,
then there exists an apartment isomorphism f′ : A′ →A fixing A′ ∩A, by Lemma 4.1.8. Let
h : A′ →Ax be the apartment isomorphism fixing A ∩A′. Then f′−1 ◦f ◦h : A′ →A′ is an
apartment isomorphism fixing Q pointwise. As it is an affine automorphism of A′, it is the
identity, which proves that f′(x) = f(x).
We denote by ρ+∞: I →A and ρ−∞: I →A the retractions centered at Q+∞and Q−∞
respectively.
Remark 4.2.8. When I is associated with a Kac–Moody group G, Proposition 4.2.3 cor-
responds to an Iwasawa decomposition of G (when V is a local face, using Lemma 6.4.1).
However, in order to prove that I is a weak masure, we will use the Iwasawa decomposition of
G (Proposition 5.2.23): our proof that (OCO) is satisfied uses Theorem 5.3.13, which uses the
Iwasawa decomposition. Therefore we do not get a fully building theoretic proof of the Iwasawa
decomposition of G.
4.3
Exchange condition
In this section, we prove that a weak masure satisfies the exchange condition.
Recall that a half-apartment is the image by an apartment isomorphism of a set of the
form D(α, k) ⊂A, with α ∈Φ and k ∈
Lambdaα. If D1, D2 are two half-apartments of an apartment A, we say that they are opposite
if D1 ∩D2 is a wall (and thus D1 ∪D2 = A).
Lemma 4.3.1. Let A and B be two distinct apartments such that A ∩B contains a half-
apartment. Then A ∩B is a half-apartment.
76


## Page 79


Proof. We can assume that A = A. Let D be a half-apartment contained in A∩B and M be its
boundary. Let α ∈Φ be such that D = α−1(R≥λ), for some λ ∈Λα. Let M = α−1({λ}). Let q
be a sector contained in D and whose boundary contains a sector-panel P of M. Let eP be the
sector-panel of M opposite to P. Let eq be the sector of D dominating eP. Let Q and eQ be the
germs at infinity of q and eq respectively. Then if x ∈A∩B, we have (x + Q)∪(x + eQ) ⊂A∩B.
Therefore S
y∈x+eQ y + Q = α−1(R≥α(x)) ⊂A ∩B, by (4.2.1).
Consequently, as B ̸= A
by assumption, we deduce the existence of a ∈R such that A ∩B ∈{α−1(R>a), α−1(R≥a)}.
Choose x ∈A such that α(x) = a. Then x+Q ⊂A∩B and hence by (OCO), x ∈x + Q ⊂A∩B
and cl∆(x+Q) ⊂A∩B. Therefore a ∈Λα and A∩B = α−1(R≥a), which proves the lemma.
Lemma 4.3.2. (see Figure 4.3.1) Let A be an apartment. Let Q, Q′ be two adjacent sector-
germs such that Q ⊂A and Q′ ̸⊂A. Let D1, D2 be two opposite half-apartments of A such
that Di ∪Q′ is contained in an apartment Ai for both i ∈{1, 2}. Let M = D1 ∩D2. Up to
renumbering, we may assume that Q ⊂D1. Then:
1. There exists a unique sector-germ Q′′ of A, not contained in D1 and adjacent to Q. This
sector-germ is adjacent to Q′.
2. A1 ∩A2 is a half-apartment of I,
3. Ai ∩A = Di for both i ∈{1, 2},
4. A ∩A1 ∩A2 = M,
5. Let D3 = A1 ∩A2. Then Ai = Di ∪D3, for both i ∈{1, 2}. In particular, if i, j ∈{1, 2}
is such that {i, j} = {1, 2}, we have Aj = (Ai \ A) ∪(A \ Ai) ∪M.
Proof. The apartment A1 contain Q and Q′. Let x ∈Int(D1). Set q = x + Q and q′ = x + Q′.
Then p = q ∩q′ is a sector-panel of A1, contained in A ∩A1. Let Q′′ be the sector-germ of
A such that x + Q′′ dominates P and Q′′ ̸= Q. If Q′′ was contained in D1, then Q, Q′ and
Q′′ would be contained in A1 and would dominate P: this is impossible. Therefore Q′′ is not
contained in D1. Moreover, Q′′ is adjacent to Q. All the other sectors adjacent to Q are
contained in D1 and hence Q′′ is unique.
The apartments A1 and A2 contain Q′ and M. Hence by Lemma 4.1.8, A1 ∩A2 contains
S
x∈M x + Q′, which is a half-apartment of both A1 and A2. Therefore A1 ∩A2 is a half-
apartment, by Lemma 4.3.1.
Let i ∈{1, 2}. By assumption Di ⊂Ai ∩A. Let j ∈{1, 2} \ {i}. Let x ∈Int(Dj). Assume
by contradiction that x ∈A ∩Ai. Then A ∩Ai is a half-apartment containing x and Di. Let
y ∈M. Then Ai contains germy(y + Q), germy(y + Q′) and germy(y + Q′′). Moreover, these
three alcoves are distinct: indeed, y ∈A1 ∩A2 ∩A. In A, germy(y +Q) and germy(y +Q′′) are
distinct, since A contains Q and Q′′. Similarly, as y ∈A1, germy(y+Q) ̸= germy(y+Q′) and as
y ∈A2, germy(y +Q′) ̸= germy(Q′′). Therefore the three alcoves germy(y +Q), germy(y +Q′)
and germy(Q′′) dominate a single panel of M: a contradiction. Therefore x /∈A ∩Ai and
A ∩Ai = Di. This proves that A ∩A1 ∩A2 = M.
Let i ∈{1, 2}. Then Di and D3 are two half-apartments of Ai. Moreover, Di∩D3 ⊂A∩A1∩
A2 = M and hence Di ∩D3 = M. Let ψ : Ai →A be an apartment isomorphism. Then ψ(Di)
and ψ(D3) are two half-apartments whose intersection is a wall. Therefore ψ(Di)∪ψ(D3) = A
and hence Di ∪D3 = Ai, which completes the proof of the lemma.
The weak masure I satisfies the exchange condition (EC) of [BS14]:
Proposition 4.3.3. (see Figure 4.3.1) Let A and B be two apartments such that A ∩B is a
half-apartment. Let M be the wall of A ∩B. Then (A \ B) ∪(B \ A) ∪M is an apartment.

Proof. Let R, R′ be two sector-germs of B of the same sign such that R ⊂A ∩B and
R′ ⊂B \ A. Let Γ = (Q0, . . . , Qk) be a gallery of sector-germs of B from R to R′. Let
i ∈J0, k −1K be minimal such that Qi ⊂A ∩B and Qi+1 ̸⊂A ∩B. We set Q = Qi and
Q′ = Qi+1. Then by the sundial configuration (SC), there exist two half-apartments D1 and
D2 of A such that for both j ∈{1, 2}, there exists an apartment Aj containing Dj ∪Q′. Let
j ∈{1, 2} be such that Dj ⊃Q. Let Q′′ be the unique sector-germ of A, not contained in Dj
and adjacent to Q. Then A ∩Aj is a half-apartment delimited containing Q but not Q′′ and
hence A ∩Aj and B ∩A are two parallel half-apartments of A. Therefore B ∩Aj contains
a half-apartment of A. Moreover B ∩Aj contains Q′ and hence B = Aj, by Lemma 4.3.1.
Therefore the lemma follows from Lemma 4.3.2.

4.4
Local sundial configuration
In this section, we prove that a weak masure satisfy the local sundial configuration. We also
study the existence of apartments containing pairs of local faces.
Proposition 4.4.1. (local sundial configuration, see Figure 4.4.1) Let D be a half-apartment
of I, P be a (local) panel contained in the wall of D and C be an alcove of I dominating P.
Then there exists an apartment A1 containing D ∪C.
Proof. Let A be an apartment containing D. If C ⊂A, it suffices to take A1 = A. We now
assume C ⊈A. Let M be the wall of D. Let x be the base-point of P. Let q be the sector of
D based at x and such that germx(q) is the alcove of D dominating P. Let Q = germ∞(q).
By Proposition 4.2.3, there exists an apartment B containing Q and C. Let q′ be the sector
of B such that germx(q′) = C. The apartment B contains x and Q and thus it contains q, by
(OCO). Moreover q′ and q dominate P and thus Q′ := germ∞(q′) and Q are adjacent.
By (SC), we can write A = D1 ∪D2, where D1, D2 are opposite half-apartments such
that Di ∪Q′ is contained in an apartment Ai for both i ∈{1, 2}. Up to renumbering, we
may assume that P ⊂A1. Then P ⊂A ∩A1. Moreover x ∈P ⊂A1 and A1 contains Q′.
Therefore A1 contains q′ and in particular, A1 contains C. Using Lemma 4.3.1, we deduce
that A ∩A1 is a half-apartment of A delimited by P. As A ∩A1 contains Q, it contains D
and thus A ∩A1 = D. Therefore A1 satisfies the condition of the proposition.

{a, b}. This proves the sense ⇒.
Let a, b ∈I be friendly. By (OCO), we can assume that Ca := Fa and Cb := Fb are two

exists an apartment A1 containing C2 and D1 and then A1 contains C2 and b. By induction,
we deduce the existence of an apartment B containing Cn = Ca and b.

4.5
Splitting of apartments
Recall that a subset of A is enclosed if it can be written as a finite intersection of half-
apartments of A. In this section, we use the sundial configuration to prove that if A is an
apartment and Q is a sector-germ, then we can decompose A as a union A = Sk
i=1 Pi, where
k ∈N and the Pi are enclosed subsets such that Pi ∪Q is contained in some apartment for all
i. This result will be important in our proof of (MA2). First, it explains why the half-spaces
considered in (MA2) are only related to real roots and not to the imaginary roots, and second,
it will enable us to prove that the image of a segment by a retraction is a Hecke path, which
is one of the ingredients of this proof. These decompositions first appeared in [Héb21].

A parametrized line of I is a map s : R →I such that there exist a, b ∈A, with a ̸= b and
ψ : A →A an apartment isomorphism, with A an apartment, such that s(t) = ψ(ta+(1−t)b),
for all t ∈R.
We deduce the following corollary, which is often proved using a compactness argument:
Corollary 4.5.3. Let A be an apartment of I and s : R →A be a parametrized line. Then
there exist k ∈N and t0, . . . , tk ∈R such that t0 < t1 < . . . < tk and such that:
1. for all i ∈J0, k −1K, s([ti, ti+1]) ∪Q is contained in an apartment,
2. s(] −∞, t0]) ∪Q (resp. s([tk, +∞[) ∪Q) is contained in an apartment.
4.6
Hecke paths
Hecke paths were introduced by Kapovich and Milson in [KM08] in the context of Euclidean
buildings, in order to study the representation theory of a reductve group over C. There were

then generalized in the frameworks of masures by Gaussent and Rousseau in [GR08]. They
are a crucial tool in the study of the representation theory of Kac–Moody groups over local
fields.
4.6.1
Definition
We now give the definition of Hecke paths. Under some additional assumption involving the
Tits preorder, Hecke paths are exactly the images of line segments by retractions (see [GR08,
Theorem 6.2 and 6.3].
Definition 4.6.1. ((W v
a , Cv)-chain) Let a ∈A and ξ, ξ′ ∈A. Let Cv be a vectorial chamber
of A. A (W v
a , Q)-chain from ξ to ξ′ with respect to Cv is a pair ((ξ0, . . . , ξk), (β1, . . . , βk)) ∈
Ak+1 × Φk such that ξ0 = ξ, ξk = ξ′ and for all i ∈J1, kK, we have:
1. rβi(ξi−1) = ξi,
2. βi(ξi−1) < 0,
3. βi(a) ∈Λβi: a belongs to a wall of direction ker(βi),
4. βi(Cv) < 0.

Definition 4.6.2. (Hecke path) A piecewise affine path γ : [0, 1] →A is a map γ : [0, 1] →

[ti, ti+1] for all i ∈J0, n −1K.

Lemma 4.6.6. Let x ∈I and A, B be two apartments containing x. We assume that A ∩B
contains an alcove C based at x. Let f : A →B be the apartment isomorphism fixing C. Then
there exists a neighborhood Ωof x in A such that f fixes A ∩B ∩Ω.
Proof. Using apartment isomorphisms, we can assume that A = A. Let Cv be the vectorial
chamber of A such that C is contained in x + Cv. Let Ω′ ∈C be such that Ω′ ⊂(A ∩B) ∩
(x + Cv). Let y ∈Ω′. Then for z ∈Ω′ close enough to x, we have z ∈y −Cv and hence up
to reducing Ω, (OCO) implies that Ω:= convA(y, Ω∩A ∩B) ⊂A ∩B and the existence of
g : A →B fixing Ω, which proves the lemma.
Lemma 4.6.7. Let A and B be two apartments such that A ∩B ̸= ∅. Let x ∈A ∩B. We
assume that there exists a half-apartment D of A such that for some neighborhood Ωof x, we
have D ∩Ω⊂A ∩B. Let P be a panel based at x and contained in the wall of D. Let C be the
alcove of A dominating P and not contained in D. Then if B ⊂C, we have x ∈Int(A ∩B).
Proof. Using apartment isomorphisms, we may assume that A = A. Let Cv be the vectorial
chamber of A such that C is contained in x+Cv. Let Ω∈C be such that Ω⊂(A∩B)∩(x+Cv).
Let y ∈Ω. Then for z ∈Ωclose enough to x, we have z ∈y −Cv and hence up to reducing
Ω, we have convA(Ω∩D, z) ⊂A ∩B, by (OCO). As convA(Ω∩D, z) contains x in its interior,
we deduce the lemma.
Lemma 4.6.8. Let x ∈I and A, B be two apartments containing x. We assume that there
exists a neighborhood Ωof x in A and a half-apartment D of A such that A∩B ⊃D∩Ω. Then
either x ∈Int(A ∩B), or there exists an apartment A′ such that A ∩A′ is a half-apartment
and x ∈Int(B ∩A′).
Proof. Reducing Ωif necessary, there exists an apartment isomorphism f : A →B fixing
Ω∩A ∩B, by Lemma 4.6.6. Let DB = f(D). Let P be a panel based at x and contained in
the wall M of D. Then P ⊂A∩B. Let C be the alcove of B dominating P and not contained
in DB. By the local sundial configuration (Proposition 4.4.1), there exists an apartment A′
containing D and C.
Then A′ ∩B contains (Ω∩DB) ∪C.
Therefore by Lemma 4.6.7,
x ∈Int(B ∩A′). If C ⊂A, then we can take A = A′. Otherwise, we have A ∩A′ = D, by
Lemma 4.3.1.
A polyhedron P of A is a non-empty finite intersection of closed half-spaces of A. We
refer to [BG09, Definition 1.3] for a definition of the faces of P. A facet of P is a proper face
of P of maximal dimension. By [BG09, Theorem 1.10], P has finitely many faces.

for all n ∈N. But then y ∈Pi, which proves that i ∈Jy. As i /∈J, we obtain that J ̸= Jy,
which proves the result.

The following theorem generalizes [GR08, Theorem 6.2].

preorder assumption on s. Our proof is also different and relies on the sundial configuration.
Theorem 4.6.13. Let s : [0, 1] →I be a segment (resp. es : R≥0 →I be a ray or a line).
Let Q be a sector-germ at infinity of A. Let ρ = ρA,Q be the retraction onto A centered at Q.
Then γ := ρ ◦s (resp. eγ = ρ ◦es) is a Hecke path (resp. a Hecke ray) of shape γ′
+(0) (resp.
eγ′
+(0)) with respect to Q.
Proof. By Corollary 4.5.3, γ and eγ are piecewise affine paths, with finitely many folding points.
By Lemma 4.6.12, we deduce the result.
4.7
A characterization of the points of A via retractions
In this section, we give a characterization of the points of A: we prove that an element x ∈I
belongs to A if and only if ρ+∞(x) = ρ−∞(x) = x (see Corollary 4.7.8).
