4.8
Convexity of the intersection of two apartments
In this section, we prove a weak version of (MA2): we prove that if A and B are two apartments,
then A ∩B is convex and there exists an apartment isomorphism from A to B fixing A ∩B.
We proceed as follows:
1. We assume that A = A and begin with the case where A ∩B has non-empty interior.
2. We reduce to the case where the Kac–Moody matrix is indecomposable.
3. We consider x, y ∈Int(A ∩B). We want to prove that [x, y]A = [x, y]B. We separate the
case where S is associated with an affine matrix and the case where the family (α∨
i )i∈I
is positively free (see Subsection 4.8.4). Note that these two cases are not disjoint.
4. In the affine case, we use the fact that “almost all pairs of points are preordered”, since
the Tits cone is almost a half space. We use (OCO) and a density argument to conclude.
5. In the positively free case, we consider an affine parametrization s : [0, 1] →[x, y]B of
[x, y]B and we study γ := ρ−∞◦s and eγ := ρ+∞◦s. Using the fact that these are Hecke
paths, we obtain inequalities (with respect to ≤Q∨
R ) satisfied by these paths. We deduce
that these two paths are necessarily the line segment of A between x and y. Using the
characterization of A given by Corollary 4.7.8, we deduce that [x, y]B ⊂A.
6. We then drop the assumption on A ∩B.
4.8.1
The intersection of two apartments is closed
We equip A with its topology of a finite dimensional affine space. The affine Weyl group W a
is a group of homeomorphisms for this topology thus we can use apartment isomorphisms to
transport this topology to each apartment.
We begin by proving that the intersection of two apartments is closed, which we use to
make density arguments.
Proposition 4.8.1. Let Q be a sector-germ of I and A be an apartment of I containing Q.
Let ρ = ρQ,A : I →A be the retraction onto A centered at Q. Let B be an apartment of I.
Then ρ|B : B →A is continuous (for the affine topologies on B and A).
Proof. Using Proposition 4.5.2, we write B = Sn
i=1 Pi where the Pi’s are closed sets of B such
that for all i ∈J1, nK, there exists an apartment Bi containing Pi and Q and an apartment
isomorphism ψi : B →Bi fixing Pi.
For all i ∈J1, nK, we denote by ϕi the apartment
isomorphism Bi →A fixing A ∩Bi, which exists by Lemma 4.1.8. Then ρ|Pi = ϕi ◦ψi|Pi for
all i ∈J1, nK.
Let (xk) ∈BN be a converging sequence and x = lim xk. For i ∈J1, nK, set Ei = {k ∈N |
xk ∈Pi}. Let i ∈J1, nK be such that Ei is infinite. Then x ∈Pi and for all k ∈Ei, we have
ρ(xk) = ϕi ◦ψi(xk)
→
k→+∞ϕi ◦ψi(x) = ρ(x). Therefore (ρ(xk))k∈N converges to ρ(x), which
proves the proposition since A and B are metric spaces.
Lemma 4.8.2. Let A and B be two apartments.
1. The set A ∩B is closed (in A or B).
2. Assume that A∩B is non-empty. Let (xn) ∈(A∩B)N. Then (xn) converges in A if and
only if (xn) converges in B and the limits are the same when (xn) converges in A or B.

Proof. (1) Without loss of generality, we can assume that A = A.
Let f+ = ρ+∞|B and
f−= ρ−∞|B. Then by Corollary 4.7.8, A ∩B = {x ∈A | f−(x) −f+(x) = 0}, which is closed
since f+ and f−are continuous (by Proposition 4.8.1). We deduce that A ∩B is closed in B
and by symmetry, A ∩B is closed in A.
(2) Let (xn) ∈(A ∩B)N. Assume that (xn) converges in A and let x be the limit of (xn)
in A. Let Q be a sector-germ of B and ρ = ρQ,B. Then (ρ(xn)) = (xn) converges to ρ(x).
As x ∈A ∩B, we have ρ(x) = x and thus when (xn) is regarded as a sequence of B, (xn)
converges to x. By symmetry, we get (2).
4.8.2
Decomposition of I
We use the definition of indecomposability for a Kac–Moody matrix given in [Kac94, Chapter
1]. We write the Kac–Moody matrix A as a diagonal block matrix whose diagonal is given by
indecomposable Kac–Moody matrices A1, . . . , Ak. For i ∈J1, kK, we denote by Ii the index set
of Ai. Then Y decomposes as Y = Lk
i=1 Yi, where Yi contains (α∨
j )j∈Ii for each i ∈J1, kK. This
induces a decomposition A = Lk
i=1 Ai, where Ai = Yi ⊗R, for i ∈J1, kK. The vectorial Weyl
group W v can be written as a direct product W v = W v
1 × . . . × W v
k , with obvious notations.
Every apartment A of I decomposes as A = A1 × . . . × Ak. This induces a decomposition
I = I1 × . . . × Ik, where for each i ∈J1, kK, Ii is a masure of type Ai.
If A = A1 × . . . × Ak and B = B1 × . . . × Bk are two apartments of I, then A ∩B =
(A1 ∩B1) × . . . × (Ak ∩Bk). Therefore if Ai ∩Bi is convex for all i ∈J1, kK, then A ∩B is
convex and hence it suffices to prove that the intersection of two apartment of each of the Ii
is convex.

Lemma 4.8.3. Let B be an apartment such that A ∩B has non-empty interior. Then A ∩B
is convex and there exists an apartment isomorphism f : B →A fixing A ∩B.
Proof. Let x ∈Int(A ∩B) and let C be an alcove of A based at x. By (OCO), there exists
f : A →B fixing C. Let Ω∈C be a convex open subset of A∩B fixed by f. Let y ∈A∩B. By
(4.8.1), there exists z ∈Ωsuch that z ≤y or z ≥y. Then by (OCO), there exists f′ : A →B
fixing [z, y]. Then (f′)−1 ◦f : A →A is an affine automorphism fixing a neighborhood of z
in [z, y] and thus it fixes the line L containing x and y. Therefore (f′)−1(f(y)) = y = f(y).
Therefore f fixes A ∩B.
Take a ∈[x, y]. Write a = tx + (1 −t)y, with t ∈[0, 1]. Let (zn) ∈ΩN be such that zn →x
and zn ≤y or zn ≥y for all n ∈N. Then tzn + (1 −t)y ∈A ∩B for all n ∈N and thus
a ∈A ∩B, by Lemma 4.8.2. Therefore A ∩B is convex.

4.9
Encloseness of the intersection of two apartments
We proved in Section 4.8 that the intersection of two apartments is convex. We now deduce

and then we deduce the result (see Theorem 4.9.6).

Theorem 4.9.6. Let S = (A, X, Y, (αi)i∈I, (α∨
i )i∈I) be a free Kac–Moody data. Let I be a
space satisfying (MA1) (SC), (OCO) and (wMA3). Then I satisfies (MA2): for all apartments
A, B, A ∩B is enclosed and there exists an apartment isomorphism from A to B fixing A ∩B.
97


## Page 100


Proof. Let A and B be two apartments. By Lemma 4.8.7, A ∩B is convex and there exists an
apartment isomorphism f : A →B fixing A ∩B. By Lemma 4.9.2 and Lemma 4.9.5, A ∩B
is enclosed and hence (MA3) is satisfied.
Remark 4.9.7.
1. Note that in [Héb22, Corollary 3.7], we proved that definition of an
abstract masure of [Rou11, Définition 2.1] is equivalent to Definition 4.1.6, when (α∨
i )
is free. Actually, by adapting the proof we gave here, we obtain that the two definitions
are equivalent, without cofreeness assumption. Indeed, the arguments of [Héb22] only use
the positively freeness, and the affine case is covered by Lemma 4.8.3.
2. In [Héb20, Proposition 4.25 and Remark 4.26], it is proved that if I is associated with
a split Kac–Moody group, then every enclosed subset of A with non-empty interior is
the intersection of two apartments of I. This seems to indicated that (MA2) is optimal.
Note that this question of which subsets of A are the intersections of two apartments is
studied in [AVM10] in the case where I is a building (spherical or affine).

Chapter 5
Parahoric subgroup associated with a
point
Let S = (A, X, Y, (αi)i∈I, (α∨
i )) be a free Kac–Moody data and G = GS be the split minimal
Kac–Moody group associated with S in Chapter 3. Let F be a field equipped with a non-
trivial valuation ω : F →R ∪{+∞}. We assume that ω(F) ⊃Z, which is possible up to
renormalization and if ω(F) is discrete, we assume that ω(F) = Z. We set G = G(F). We
mainly use gothic letters to denote the subfunctors of G: B±, U±, T, N, ... and we use roman
letters to denote their evaluations at F: B± = B±(F), U± = U±(F), T = T(F), N = N(F),
...
We want to define the masure of (G, ω). We use the same methods as in [BT72] (in the
reductive case), [GR08] and [Rou16]:
1. Let T be a maximal split torus of G and T = T(F). Let Y be the cocharacter lattice
of (G, T) and let N be the normalizer of T in G. We define the standard apartment
of the masure as A = Y ⊗R. There is a natural action νv of N on A by linear maps
(and independant of the valuation on F). We define an action ν of N on A by affine
automorphisms such that for each n ∈N, νv(n) is the linear part of ν(n).
2. We define for each x ∈A its fixator Gx in the (not yet defined) masure. If x ∈A, Gx is
defined as the subgroup of G generated by U+
x,∞, U−
x,∞and Nx, where Nx is the fixator
of x in N and U+
x,∞is defined as the “fixator” of x in U+ (and similarly for U−
x,∞). The
group U+
x,∞is defined via the coordinates Xα, α ∈∆+ defined in Subsection 3.2.3 (see
Definition 5.2.4 or Proposition 5.2.5).
3. The masure I is then defined as (G × A)/ ∼, where ∼is an equivalence relation defined
in such a way that Gx is the fixator of x in G, for x ∈A.
Note that eventually, we obtain that Gx can be defined similarly as in the reductive case:
Gx = ⟨Uα,x | α ∈Φ, Nx⟩(see Proposition 7.1.7). This definition involves the minimal
group only and is simpler. We could in principle take it as a definition. However, we
use the masure to prove this result and we do not know how to prove that Gx and the
masure have “good properties” without using the maximal groups.
In this chapter, we define the parahoric subgroups Gx, for x ∈A.
Note that we change the notation compared to [GR08] and [Rou16]: if V is a filter on A, we
write UV,fin instead of UV, U±
V,fin instead of U±
V , U+

In Section 5.1, we define the vectorial and affine actions on N on A.

5.1
Action of N on A
Recall that as a set, the standard apartment is A = Y ⊗R, where Y is the cocharacter lattice
of S. In this section, we define an action of N = N(F) on A by affine automorphisms. We
start by defining an action νv of N(R) on A by linear maps, for any ring R. We then define
an action of N on A by affine automorphisms, which takes the valuation ω into account and
such that the linear part of ν(n) is νv(n), for all n ∈N.

inf eΛ(E) ∈R, then E admits a minimum.

5.2.4
The Iwasawa decomposition
In this subsection, we prove the Iwasawa decomposition. It enables to define retractions with
respect to sector-germs in the masure, which are an important tool in the study of Kac–Moody
groups. It also enables to prove that the Gx, that we define in Section 5.3, decompose as the
product of their U+, U−and N components (see Theorem 5.3.13), which is a crucial property
in the study of masures.
For α ∈Φ, we set:
G(α) = ⟨T, Uα, U−α⟩⊂G.
By (KMT7) ((3.1.13), if α = w.αi, with w ∈W v and i ∈I, we have:
G(α) = ewG(αi) ew−1.
Let Si = ((2), X, Y, (α∨
i ){i}, (αi){i}). Then Si is a Kac–Moody data. The inclusion Si →S is
a Kac–Moody data morphism in the sense of [Rou16, 1.1 6) & 7)] and S is an extension of
Si. By [Rou16, 1.10], this induces an injective functor morphism GSi →G. Then GSi(F) is
canonically isomorphic to ⟨T, Uαi, U−αi⟩, by [Mar18, Before Proposition 7.75] and hence we
can regard G(αi) as the Kac–Moody group associated with Si.
Definition 5.2.21. We say that V is almost-open if:
∀α ∈Φ, fV(α) + fV(−α) > 0.
We say that V is narrow if no wall separates V in two, i.e: for all α ∈Φ, we have:
1. fV(α) + fV(−α) ∈{0, 0+} or in the discrete case, if fV(α) + fV(−α) ∈{0, 1},
2. (fV(α), fV(−α)) /∈{(λ+, (−λ)+) | λ ∈Λ}.

Note that the Iwasawa decomposition has a masure theoretic interpretation : it corre-
sponds to the existence of apartments containing certain pairs of filters, see Remark 6.4.5. By
Lemma 5.2.24 below, this proposition is actually a consequence of Proposition 4.2.3, once we
know that the masure of a Kac–Moody group is a masure in the sense of Definition 4.1.6.
However, we will use the Iwasawa decomposition in order to prove that I is an abstract ma-
sure: we use it in the proof of Theorem 5.3.13, which gives a decomposition of the fixator Gx
of an element x of A.
Lemma 5.2.24. Let V be a narrow filter on A. Then V is inseparable.
Proof. First assume that we are in the non-discrete case. Let α ∈Φ. By definition of narrow-
ness (see Definition 5.2.21), fV(α) ∈R or fV(−α) ∈R. Up to considering −α instead of α,
we can assume that a := fV(α) ∈R. Then we have V ⊂D(α, a) and V ⊂D(−α, −b), for all
b ∈R such that b < fV(−α). Therefore V ⊂D(−α, −b) for all b ∈] −∞, a] and V ⊂D(α, b)
for all b ∈[a, +∞[. Therefore V is inseparable.
Assume now that Λ = Z. If fV(α) = −fV(−α), we have V ⊂D(α, b) for all b ∈[fV(α), +∞[
and V ⊂D(−α, −b) for all b ∈]−∞, fV(α)]. Assume fV(α) = −fV(−α)+1. Then V ⊂D(α, b),
for all b ∈[fV(α), +∞[ and for all b ∈Z such that b < fV(α), we have b ≤fV(α)−1 and hence
V ⊂D(−α, 1 −fV(α)) = D(−α, fV(−α)) ⊂D(−α, −b). Therefore V is inseparable.
5.3
Parahoric subgroup associated with a point
For x ∈A, we set Gx = ⟨U+
x,∞, U−
x,∞, Nx⟩. A crucial property of Gx is that
Gx = U+

x,∞· Nx = U−
x,∞· U+

(see Theorem 5.3.13). The aim of this subsection is to prove this property.
Compared to the reductive case, a difficulty is that the definition of Gx involves Gma+ and
Gma−. Moreover, in the reductive case, ∆is finite and thus we can make inductions, using
commutation relations between Uα,x and Uβ,x, for α, β ∈Φ. In the Kac–Moody case, ∆is
infinite and thus it is difficult to apply these methods. In order to study Gx, we interpret it
via the action of G on modules. This interpretation will not be used elsewhere in the paper
and the reader ready to admit (5.3.1) can skip this section.

5.3.2
Highest weight modules
In this subsection, we recollect some facts on the highest weight modules that we use in the
definition of the Gx.
Recall the definition of n+ in 2.2.2.2.
Definition 5.3.3. A g-module V is called a highest-weight module with highest weight
λ ∈h∗if there exists a non-zero vector vλ ∈V (called a highest-weight vector) such that
1. n+.vλ = 0 and h.vλ = λ(h)vλ, for all h ∈h,
2. UC(g).vλ = V .
Let V be a highest-weight module with highest-weight λ and highest weight vector vλ.
Note that by (3.1.1), if α ∈Q+ and u ∈U−α,C, we have u.vλ ∈Vλ−α. Therefore a highest-
weight module V is h-diagonalizable: V = L
α∈Q+ Vλ−α and Vλ = U0,C.vλ = h.vλ = Cvλ is
one-dimensional. More generally, if ν ∈Q and µ ∈h∗, we have Uν,C.Vµ ⊂Vµ+ν.
A highest-weight g-module M(λ) with highest-weight λ is called a Verma module if
every highest-weight g-module with highest weight λ is a quotient of M(λ).
By [Mar18,
Lemma 4.9], for every λ ∈h∗, there exists a unique (up to isomorphism) Verma module
M(λ). Moreover, M(λ) contains a unique proper maximal submodule M′(λ). One then sets
L(λ) = M(λ)/M ′(λ). By [Mar18, Proposition 4.11], L(λ) is an integrable g-module (in the

## Page 123


Chapter 6
Study of the masure associated with a
split Kac–Moody group over a valued
field
Recall that G = G(F), where G is a split minimal Kac–Moody group and F is a field equipped
with a non-trivial valuation ω : F →R. In this chapter, we define the masure of G and we
prove that it is a abstract masure in the sense of Definition 4.1.6. To that end, we proceed as
follows:
1. We define I as G × A/ ∼, for some equivalence relation ∼on G × A defined in such a
way that the fixator of every element x of A is Gx, as defined in Section 5.3.
2. We prove that I satisfies (OCO). To that end, we use the notion of “good fixators”
introduced in [GR08]: a filter is said to have a good fixator if its fixator satisfies certain
decompositions (see Definition 6.2.1). Actually, we will see that every filter has a good
fixator (see Theorem 6.6.7), but this notion will be useful to prove (OCO).
3. We prove that I satisfies (SC).
4. We prove that I satisfies (wMA3) and (MA3): (wMA3) is obtained by translating the
(vectorial) Bruhat and the Birkhoff decompositions of G in terms of masures and (MA3)
is obtained by using Levi decompositions and the affine Bruhat decomposition in certain
reductive groups.
5. We obtain that I satisfies (MA2) by using Theorem 4.9.6.
6.1
Definition of the masure associated with G
We define an equivalence relation ∼on G × A as follows: (g, x) ∼(h, y) if and only if there
exists n ∈N such that y = ν(n).x and g−1.h.n ∈Gx. The masure I = I(G, T, F, ω) is the
set G × A/ ∼. For (g, x) ∈G × A, we denote by [g, x] or by g.x its image in I, depending on
the context. We define an action of G on I by g.[g′, x] = [gg′, x] if g, g′ ∈G and x ∈A.
By definition, we have [n, x] = [1, ν(n).x], for all x ∈A and n ∈N. Therefore N stabilizes
A and the action on A induced by the action on I coincides with the one of ν. By definition
also, the map ι : A →I defined by ι(x) = [1, x] is injective. It enables to regard A as a subset
of I.
An apartment of I is then a set of the form g.A, for some g ∈G.
If V is a filter on A, we define:
G(V ↷A) = {g ∈G | g.V ⊂A}.
This subgroup is denoted G(V ⊂A) in [Rou16].

6.2
Filters with good fixators
In this section, we introduce the notion of filters having a good fixator. This will be useful to
prove axiom (OCO). We will eventually prove that every filter on A has a good fixator (see
Theorem 6.6.7).

Corollary 6.3.10. The fixator GA of A in G is T0 = T(O).

Lemma 6.3.11. Let V be a filter on A. Then V has a transitive fixator (i.e satisfies (TF)) if
and only if GV acts transitively on the set of apartments containing V.
Proof. (⇒) Assume that V has a transitive fixator. Let B be an apartment containing V and
let h ∈G be such that h.B = A. Then h ∈G(V ↷A) and hence we can write h = ng, with
n ∈N and g ∈GV. Then g.B = n−1h.B = n−1.A = A and g fixes V, which proves that GV
acts transitively on the apartments containing V.
(⇐) Assume that GV acts transitively on the set of apartments containing V and take an
apartment B containing V. Let g ∈G(V ↷A). Let B = g.A. Then B is an apartment
containing V and thus there exists h ∈G such that h.A = B and h ∈GV. Then h−1g.A = A
and hence h−1g ∈N, by Proposition 6.3.9. Therefore g ∈N · GV, which proves that V has a
transitive fixator.

case of Proposition 4.2.3, once we know that I is a weak masure.

infinity. Let A be an apartment containing Q1. Then there exists two opposite half-apartments

6.5.4). Therefore by Theorem 4.9.6, I satisfies (MA2). Using Lemma 6.3.11, we deduce that
every filter on A satisfies (TF).

Lemma 6.6.1. Let g ∈G. We assume that g fixes a subset Ωof A such that Ωhas non-empty
interior. Then g fixes cl(Ω). Moreover, if B = g.A, then g fixes A ∩A.
Proof. Let B = g.A. Let h ∈G be such that h.A = B and such that h fixes A ∩B, which
exists by (MA2). Then h−1g.A = A and h−1g acts as an affine automorphism on A. As it fixes
Ω, which has non-empty interior, it is the identity. Moreover A ∩B is enclosed (by (MA2))
and contains Ω, so A ∩B ∈cl(Ω) and h fixes A ∩B. Therefore g fixes A ∩B, which proves
the lemma.

Chimneys and axiom (MA3)
In this section, we prove that I satisfies axiom (MA3). We start by describing the fixator of a
chimney and of its germ (see Proposition 6.7.9). We then prove a decomposition of G, which
implies (MA3) (see Proposition 6.7.11).
6.7.1
Masure associated with a set of simple roots
Let J ⊂I. Let ΦJ = (L
i∈J Rαi) ∩Φ. By [Mar18, 7.4], the group GJ = ⟨T, Uα | α ∈ΦJ⟩
naturally identifies with GS|J(F), where S|J = ((ai,j)i,j∈J, X, Y, (αi)i∈J, (α∨
i )i∈J).
Let AJ
be the set A equipped with the hyperplane arrangement {α−1({k}) | k ∈Λ, α ∈ΦJ}. The
group GJ acts on two masures: the masure IJ = I(GS|J, F, ω), whose standard apartment
is AJ, and the “masure” GJ.A ⊂I. We prove in this subsection that they can be identified.
In order to distinguish these two masures, we denote by g ⊙x the element of IJ, which
is the translate of x by g.
We add an index J to denote the objects relative to IJ: for
example U+
J = ⟨Uα | α ∈Φ+ ∩ΦJ⟩, U+
x,J denotes ⟨Uα,x | α ∈ΦJ⟩∩U+, etc. Note that for
n ∈NJ = ⟨T, esj | j ∈J⟩and x ∈AJ, we have n ⊙x = n.x since these actions are defined
similarly.
Lemma 6.7.1. Let x ∈A. Then GJ ∩Gx = Gx,J, where Gx,J is the fixator of x in (GJ, ⊙).
Proof. By the Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt theorem, the embedding gJ ,→g induces an embedding
of their envelopping algebras UC(gJ) ,→UC(g).
This induces an embedding UJ ,→U, by
definition of these Z-forms and thus we can regard UJ and UJ,F′ = UJ ⊗F′ as sub-algebras
of U and UF′ = U ⊗F′ respectively (where F′ is defined in Notation 5.3.4). Moreover, by
definition of the adjoint representation (see (3.1.17)), we have AdF′,J(u)(v) = AdF′(u)(v), for
u, v ∈UJ,F′. By restriction, every highest weight module for g is a highest weight module for
gJ. Conversely, by [Mar18, Lemma 4.9] and its proof, every gJ-highest weight module can
be extended to a g-highest weight module. Thus if MJ is the analog of M for gJ (for the
notation of Subsection 5.3.4), then we have a natural bijection M →MJ, M 7→M, which
associates to each M its restriction to gJ. By [Mar18, Theorem 7.48], MJ is simply the set
M, where each M is equipped with the restriction of M to GJ. Therefore Gx,J = {g ∈GJ |
g.Mx = Mx, ∀M ∈M} = Gx ∩GJ.
Proposition 6.7.2. The map ι : IJ →GJ.A defined by g ⊙x 7→g.x, for g ∈GJ and x ∈AJ
is well-defined and is a bijection.
Proof. Let (g, x), (h, y) ∈GJ ×AJ be such that g⊙x = h⊙y. Then h−1g⊙x = y. Therefore by
definition of IJ, there exists n ∈NJ such that y = n ⊙x and n−1g ∈Gx,J. By Lemma 6.7.1,
n−1h−1g.x = x and g.x = hn.x = h.y. This proves that ι is well-defined and it is clearly
surjective (since AJ = A as a set).
Using the Iwasawa decomposition in GJ (Proposition 5.2.23), we write g = u+nk, with
u+ ∈U+
J , n ∈NJ and k ∈Gx,J. Then y = g.x = ρ+∞(g.x) = n.x. Therefore n−1g ∈
GJ ∩Gx = Gx,J (by Lemma 6.7.1). Thus n−1g ⊙x = x and hence g ⊙x = n ⊙x = n.x = y.
This proves that ι is injective and completes the proof of the proposition.
Corollary 6.7.3. Let x ∈A and g ∈Gx ∩GJ.
Then g fixes pointwise x + VJ, where
VJ = T
i∈J ker(αi).

Corollary 6.7.12. The masure I of G satisfies axiom (MA3): if R1 is a splayed chimney
germ and if R2 is a chimney germ or a local face, then there exists an apartment containing
R1 and R2.
Proof. If R2 is a chimney germ, then this is a consequence of Proposition 6.7.11 and of
Lemma 6.4.1.
Assume now that R2 = F is a face.
Up to translating by an element of
G, we can assume that F is contained in A. Let r2 = r(F, Aines). Then r2 is a chimney. Thus
there exists an apartment A containing R1 and the germ R′
2 of r2 at infinity. Then A contains
R1 and F + ξ, for some ξ ∈Aines. By (MA2), A ∩A is a finite intersection of half-apartments
and thus A∩A contains F +ξ +Aines = F +Aines, which proves that A contains F and R1.

The Iwahori subgroup
By [Mar18, Proposition 8.128], G is a non-degenerate Tits functor. In particular, the natural
projection projk : O →k induces a projection projk : Gmin(O) →G(k). We now obtain the
usual description of the Iwahori subgroup in the Kac–Moody frameworks.
